
 
 
 Asynchronous BFT Asset Transfer: Quasi-Anonymous, Light, and Consensus-Free

  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

 Asynchronous BFT Asset Transfer: 
 Quasi-Anonymous, Light, and Consensus-Free 
 
 
 Timothé Albouy
 
 Affiliation:   Mathieu Gestin,
 Arthur Rauch,
 Michel Raynal,
 François Taïani
 
 
    
 Emmanuelle Anceaume
 
 Affiliation:  
 
    
 Davide Frey
 
 Affiliation:  Univ Rennes, Inria, CNRS, IRISA,
35042 Rennes-cedex, France
 

 Abstract 
    
 This paper introduces a new asynchronous Byzantine-tolerant asset transfer system (cryptocurrency) with three noteworthy properties: quasi-anonymity, lightness, and consensus-freedom. Quasi-anonymity means no information is leaked regarding the receivers and amounts of the asset transfers. Lightness means that the underlying cryptographic schemes are  succinct  ( i.e. , they produce short-sized and quickly verifiable proofs) and each process only stores its own transfers while keeping communication cost as low as possible. Consensus-freedom means the system does not rely on a total order of asset transfers. The proposed algorithm is the first asset transfer system that simultaneously fulfills all these properties in the presence of asynchrony and Byzantine processes. To obtain them, the paper adopts a modular approach combining a new distributed object called “agreement proof” and well-known techniques such as commitments, universal accumulators, and zero-knowledge proofs. 
    
 Keywords:  Anonymity, Asset transfer, Asynchrony, Byzantine Fault Tolerance, Consensus-freedom, Cryptography, Distributed computing, Lightness.

 1  Introduction 

 
 Imagine a world without the ability to send money instantly across the globe, a world where financial borders seemed impassable.
This was our world just a few decades ago.
The advent of online money transfers, a real silent revolution, has turned our lives upside down and is reshaping the global economic landscape.
Its potential for a positive impact on the world ( e.g. , decentralized and secured financial transactions, democratized access to financial services, micro-payments) is far from exhausted.
Existing decentralized money transfer, also called  decentralized or distributed asset transfer ,
faces a range of challenges pertaining to privacy ( i.e. , transfer confidentiality), performance ( i.e. , throughput and latency), and communication/computational/storage costs.
Addressing these challenges amounts to designing a system that enjoys: 
 
 •  
 
 Anonymity  to hide both the identity of the parties involved in the transfer ( i.e. , the sender and/or the recipient) 1 1 
                   1 
                  
                  
                  
                  
                  
                  
                  
                Note that when the anonymity of only the sender or the recipient is hidden, we talk about quasi-anonymity.  and the amount of transferred assets, 
 
 •  
 
 Lightness  to maintain low operational costs in terms of computation (in particular, ensuring the  succinctness  of security proofs), storage (each participant only needs to store her own transfer history), and communication ( i.e. , low bit complexity), 
 
 •  
 
 Consensus freedom  to operate deterministically in an asynchronous failure-prone setting. 
 
 
 
 
 Unfortunately, existing systems invariably satisfy only a subset of these three important properties. 
 
 
 First, until recently, all asset-transfer systems relied on a consensus primitive.
Consensus was thought necessary to avoid double spending, but as shown in  [ 1 ,  26 ,  27 ] , a reliable broadcast primitive whose deliveries respect process sending order is sufficient when each account is owned by a single process.
This has direct practical implications because consensus imposes a non-required sequential processing of all the asset transfers (or block by block in the case of blockchains).
On the other hand, not only can consensus-free asset-transfer systems process transfers concurrently, resulting in higher throughput  [ 2 ,  14 ] , but they also make it possible to leverage deterministic algorithms that achieve progress in asynchronous settings.
In practical systems, this means not having to wait for synchrony assumptions to be satisfied, a condition that would otherwise lead to long delays in large-scale systems.
The interested reader may find possibility and impossibility results for payment channels in asynchronous asset transfer in  [ 37 ] . 
 
 
 Second, even if recent years have shown the emergence of a variety of consensus-free solutions for asset transfer, none of them, except for the recent Zef  [ 3 ]  feature any level of anonymity.
Anonymous asset transfer systems typically rely on consensus to enable multiple users to hide behind a set of accounts or tokens.
Indeed, recent work  [ 22 ]  has shown that consensus is necessary to guarantee full anonymity in a system where asset transfers from correct processes never fail. Zef  [ 3 ]  circumvents this impossibility by weakening the anonymity requirement, similar to what we do in this paper. 
 
 
 Finally, most systems (including Zef  [ 3 ] ) exhibit high storage and/or communication cost as summarized in Table  1 .
With respect to storage, most systems require nodes to store the entire history of all asset transfers ( e.g. , the entire blockchain) or at least a number of transfers proportional to this entire history. With respect to communication, they feature a cost that is at least linear in the number of nodes, and quadratic in the case of Zef (Table  1 ). 
 
 
 Combining quasi-anonymity, lightness, and consensus freedom.   
The challenge addressed in this paper consists in designing the first asset transfer system that achieves anonymity, and consensus-freedom while incurring as-low-as-possible storage and communication costs. Specifically, the proposed solution achieves the following properties. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 Quasi-anonymity:  it hides the amount and the receiver’s identity of each asset transfer. 
 
 2.  
 
 Lightness: 
All the cryptographic schemes it uses are succinct— i.e. , with at most polylogarithmic proof size and verification time—the storage cost incurred by each process  p i p_{i}  is linear in the number of transfers of  p i p_{i}  for a fixed security parameter  λ \lambda , and the communication cost of the algorithm remains as low as possible. 
 
 3.  
 
 Consensus freedom:  The proposed solution consists of a deterministic algorithm that can operate in an asynchronous setting prone to failures, thereby supporting responsive implementations. 
 
 
 
 
 Roadmap.   
This paper is made up of  7 
main sections.
 Section   2  positions the contribution with respect to the state of the art.
 Section   3  defines the computing system model.
 Section   4  provides a concurrent specification of quasi-anonymous asset transfer (QAAT).
 Section   5  informally defines the cryptographic schemes used in our system.
 Section   6  presents a novel
 Agreement Proof  abstraction, our QAAT system, and the intuition behind its correctness.
Finally,  Section   7  concludes the article. 
 
 
 Mote technical developments, such as the proofs of
the QAAT algorithm, and the properties and implementations of  Section   5 ’s s
chemes appear in appendices. 
 
 
 
 2  Background and State of the Art 

 
 As we briefly mentioned above, some notable systems have addressed each of the challenges we presented individually, but to the best of our knowledge, we are the first to address all of them in a single system.

 Anonymous and quasi-anonymous asset transfer systems.   
Anonymous Asset Transfer (AAT) systems have been studied since the introduction of the online payment system Bitcoin in  2008 2008   [ 36 ]  and can be categorized as either  token-based  or  account-based .
The two types of systems differ by the persistency of the financial medium. A token can only be used once while an account has a recorded balance.
To transfer a token, the sender must prove that it possesses it and that this token has never been used before.
This is generally achieved by maintaining a list of unspent tokens and a list of spent tokens.
Anonymity is typically obtained by relying on cryptographic primitives (Zero-knowledge proofs—ZKPs) that allow one party to convince another party that a given statement is true without divulging any information beyond the fact that the statement is true.
An example of token-based systems is Zcash  [ 28 ] . 
 
 
 Asset transfer via accounts requires the sender to prove that its account has sufficient funds.
Anonymity is guaranteed by having the sender select at random a subset of its accounts.
The Quisquis  [ 19 ]  and the Zether  [ 11 ]  asset-transfer systems represent account-based AATs. 
 
 
 All the systems we mentioned  [ 28 ,  19 ,  11 ]  provide full anonymity ( i.e. , they ensure both sender and receiver anonymity), but they also rely on consensus and therefore cannot be implemented deterministically in an asynchronous failure-prone system.
Indeed, it was recently shown  [ 22 ]  that fully anonymous asset transfer requires consensus or equivalently total order to be implemented, if asset transfers from a correct process must never fail. 
 
 
 We show in the this paper that guaranteeing quasi-anonymity in place of anonymity is sufficient to circumvent this impossibility result, similar to what is done by Zef  [ 3 ] . 
 
 
 Light asset transfer systems.   
The idea of lightness consists in guaranteeing that all underlying cryptographic schemes are  succinct , that processes only store transactions they are involved in, and that communication cost remains as low as possible.
A cryptographic proving scheme is succinct if and only if its proof size and verification time are at most polylogarithmic in the “size of the problem”  [ 6 ] , where the size of the problem depends on the scheme. 3 3 
             3 
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          Following the literature, we only consider the internal state and the cryptographic schemes used by our system when analyzing its storage cost.
In particular, the storage cost induced by the network routing protocol is ignored.
 
For instance, it may refer to the number of signatures in an aggregated signature scheme  [ 8 ] , or to the arithmetic circuit size used in a Succinct Non-interactive Argument scheme (SNARG).
Intuitively, succinctness captures the fact that a practicable system has to use cryptographic implementations that are themselves practicable ( i.e. , their verification and storage costs do not become prohibitively high with time). 
 
 
 Reducing the local storage cost of decentralized asset transfer systems has been a research challenge since the advent of the Bitcoin blockchain. The notion of light clients, implemented with the simplified payment verification (SPV) protocol  [ 36 ]  allows clients to only store block headers and Merkle proofs in place of full blocks.
Still, their local storage grows linearly with the size of the blockchain.
Other solutions, such as the one presented in  [ 12 ] , allow a succinct and secure construction of proofs,  i.e. , Merkle mountain ranges, but still require the full blockchain to be maintained to update the proof with new blocks.
In contrast, non-interactive proofs of proof-of-work succeed in constructing secure and succinct token-based asset transfer systems by sampling a polylogarithmic number of blocks of the blockchain  [ 29 ,  30 ]  but do not target full anonymity of the transfers.
Finally, the Mina system  [ 9 ]  leverages SNARKs (Succinct Non-interactive Arguments of Knowledge) to obtain a succinct blockchain.
However, their construction requires maintaining the full knowledge of all the accounts of the system to update the proof when a transfer occurs.
In addition, Mina does not provide any level of anonymity for the transfers.
Those solutions do not explicitly say that they rely on P2P for their communication. Assuming that this is the case, sending a piece of information costs  n ​ log ⁡ ( n ) n\log(n)  messages in average. 
 
 
 Consensus-free asset transfer systems.   
Fundamentally, an asset-transfer system must be double-spending-free; that is, spending a unit of value more than once must be impossible.
This means that all parties agree on the order in which transfers are issued from each individual account: if some account issues two conflicting transfers spending the same funds, the rest of the network, and especially the corresponding creditors, have to agree on which transfer (if any) is correct.
This relaxed, per-account ordering of transfers can be obtained by Byzantine-tolerant communication primitives weaker than consensus,  e.g. ,  Byzantine-tolerant reliable broadcast   [ 10 ] . 4 4 
             4 
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          This is only the case for asset transfer systems where each account is owned by a single process.
In systems where accounts can have multiple owners, total ordering is needed  [ 26 ,  27 ] ,  i.e. , consensus or atomic broadcast.
 
Following  [ 26 ] , several works have proposed payment systems based on reliable broadcast only, namely AFRT20  [ 1 ] , Astro  [ 14 ] , and FastPay  [ 2 ] .
Astro was later extended to permissionless environments with Pastro  [ 33 ] , combining weighted quorums and proof-of-stake.
Building on FastPay, Zef  [ 3 ]  provides quasi-anonymous transfers, but it incurs quadratic communication cost and storage cost linear in the total number of transfers. 
 
 
 
 3  System Model 

 
 Processes.   
The system comprises  n n  sequential asynchronous processes denoted by  p 1 , … , p n p_{1},...,p_{n} .
Each process  p i p_{i}  has a unique identity, which is known to other processes. To simplify the presentation without loss of generality, we assume that  i i  is the identity of  p i p_{i} .
Up to  t < n / 3 t<n/3  processes can be Byzantine, where a Byzantine process is a process whose behavior may not follow the algorithm  [ 34 ,  38 ] . 5 5 
             5 
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          The assumption  t < n / 3 t<n/3  is only needed for the  Agreement Proof  scheme of our system (see  Section   6.1 ). 
Byzantine processes may collude to fool non-Byzantine (a.k.a. correct) processes. 
 
 
 Network.   
Processes communicate by exchanging messages through a fully connected asynchronous point-to-point communication network, which is assumed to be reliable ( i.e. , the network does not corrupt, drop, duplicate, or create messages).
Let  msg  be a message type and  v v  the value contained in the message.
The operation “ 𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{send}   msg ​ ( v ) \textsc{msg}(v)   to   p j p_{j} ” is used for sending, and the callback “ when   msg ​ ( v ) \textsc{msg}(v)   is   𝗋𝖾𝖼𝖾𝗂𝗏𝖾𝖽 \mathsf{received} ” is used for receiving.
For syntactic sugar, processes can also communicate using the macro-operation denoted  𝖻𝗋𝗈𝖺𝖽𝖼𝖺𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{broadcast}   msg ​ ( v ) \textsc{msg}(v) , that is a shorthand for “ for all   j ∈ { 1 , ⋯ , n } j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}   do   𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{send}   msg ​ ( v ) \textsc{msg}(v)   to   p j p_{j} ”.
When processes use this macro-operation to disseminate a message, we say that this message is  broadcast  and  received .
The macro-operation  𝖻𝗋𝗈𝖺𝖽𝖼𝖺𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{broadcast}   msg ​ ( v ) \textsc{msg}(v)  is unreliable.
For example, if the invoking process crashes during its invocation, an arbitrary subset of processes receives the message  msg ​ ( v ) \textsc{msg}(v) .
Moreover, due to its very nature, a Byzantine process can send conflicting messages without using the macro-operation  𝖻𝗋𝗈𝖺𝖽𝖼𝖺𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{broadcast} .
Finally, the processes have access to  𝗋𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{ra\_send} / 𝗋𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗋𝖾𝖼𝖾𝗂𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ra\_receive}  operations (for “receiver-anonymous send/receive”), which function like the classical  𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{send} / 𝗋𝖾𝖼𝖾𝗂𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{receive}  operations with the additional guarantees that  (i)  the message cannot be read by anyone other than the receiver, and that  (ii)  the receiver remains anonymous from an adversary eavesdropping the network.
For instance, these two operations could be implemented by broadcasting an encrypted message to the whole network that only the intended receiver can decrypt, or by using onion routing  [ 39 ] . 6 6 
             6 
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          Note that both these solutions have a communication complexity of at most  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n) . 
 
 
 Cryptographic schemes.   
In this paper, we use cryptographic schemes such as secure hash functions, asymmetric signatures, commitments, accumulators, and arguments of knowledge.
In the following, we specify these schemes regarding abstract operations and guarantees.
We provide concrete implementation examples in  Appendix   C , detailing how each choice constrains the adversary’s power. 
 
 
 Security parameter  λ \bm{\lambda} .   
We denote by  λ \lambda  the security parameter of the cryptographic schemes used in our algorithms.
There is a trade-off between the security level of our cryptographic schemes (represented by
 λ \lambda ) and their computational, communication, and storage costs.
We further assume that  λ \lambda  dominates  log ⁡ n \log n ,  i.e. ,  λ = Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) \lambda=\Omega(\log n) . 
 
 
 Different notions of adversaries.   
We consider an adversary denoted  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  with both  distributed  and  cryptographic  aspects.
The distributed aspect seeks to compromise the correctness of the system by controlling process faults (Byzantine faults in our case) and the scheduling of messages (asynchrony).
The cryptographic aspect, on the other hand, strives to compromise the anonymity properties of the system.
In this respect, we consider a  probabilistic polynomial-time  (PPT) adversary,  i.e. , one that has bounded computational power.
It has only access to the information publicly transiting on the network ( i.e. , the messages communicated using the clear-text primitives  𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{send} ,  𝖻𝗋𝗈𝖺𝖽𝖼𝖺𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{broadcast} , and  𝗋𝖾𝖼𝖾𝗂𝗏𝖾𝖽 \mathsf{received} ), not the messages privately exchanged between pairs of processes using the  𝗋𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{ra\_send} / 𝗋𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗋𝖾𝖼𝖾𝗂𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ra\_receive}  operations defined before. 
 
 
 Notations.   
The  invocation  by a process of an operation  𝗈𝗉 \mathsf{op}  with input parameter  𝑖𝑛 \mathit{in}  and output result  𝑜𝑢𝑡 \mathit{out}  is denoted
 𝗈𝗉 ⁡ ( 𝑖𝑛 ) / 𝑜𝑢𝑡 \mathsf{op}(\mathit{in})/\mathit{out} .
The “ ⋆ \star ” symbol means that the corresponding value is left unspecified,  e.g. ,  𝗈𝗉 i ​ ( ⋆ ) \mathsf{op}_{i}(\star)  refers to an invocation of  𝗈𝗉 \mathsf{op}  by  p i p_{i}  with an arbitrary input.
A pair made up of  a a  and  b b  is denoted  ⟨ a , b ⟩ \langle a,b\rangle .
 Table   2  summarizes key notations used throughout this paper.

 4  Quasi-Anonymous Asset Transfer (QAAT): Concurrent Specification 

 
 Asset Transfer  ( AT ), and its extension,  Quasi-Anonymous Asset Transfer  ( QAAT ) are derived from the  concurrent  asset transfer specification of AFRT20  [ 1 ] .
We refer to this specification as concurrent because it does not consider asset transfer as a sequential object handling operation invocations in a total order.
In the following, we assume that each account is owned by a single process. 
 
 
 AT operations.   
A distributed AT object provides processes with two operations: 
 
 •  
 
 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ​ ( ) / v \mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v  returns the account balance  v v  of the calling process  p i p_{i}  according to its current local vision. 
 
 •  
 
 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , j ) / r \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j)/r  transfers the amount  v v  from the account of the calling process  p i p_{i}  to the account of  p j p_{j} , returns  r = 𝚌𝚘𝚖𝚖𝚒𝚝 r=\mathtt{commit}  if the account of  p i p_{i}  has enough funds,  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise.
For simplicity, if  r = 𝚌𝚘𝚖𝚖𝚒𝚝 r=\mathtt{commit} , we omit the writing of the result of the corresponding invocation:  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , j ) / 𝚌𝚘𝚖𝚖𝚒𝚝 \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j)/\mathtt{commit}  can be simply written  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j) . 
 
 
 
 
 The above  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 ⁡ ( ) \mathsf{balance}()  operation is weaker than that of AFRT20  [ 1 ]  as it can only return the balance of the local process, and not of any process of the system.
This is needed to ensure confidentiality (since a given process should not be able to read the accounts of other processes). 
 
 
 It is assumed that the account of each process  p i p_{i}  is initialized to a nonnegative value denoted  𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i \mathtt{init}_{i}  (in our QAAT implementation of  Algorithms   1 – 3 ,  𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i \mathtt{init}_{i}  is known only by  p i p_{i} ). 
 
 
 Histories and sequences.    
 
 •  
 
 Let  S = ( o k ) k ∈ [ 1 . . | S | ] S=(o_{k})_{k\in[1..|S|]}  be a sequence of operation invocations.  S S  might be finite (in which case  | S | ∈ ℕ |S|\in\mathbb{N} ), or infinite (in which case  | S | = + ∞ |S|=+\infty , and  [ 1 . . | S | ] = ℕ [1..|S|]=\mathbb{N} ). We note  𝗌𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( S ) \mathsf{set}(S)  the set of invocations in  S S ,  → S \rightarrow_{S}  the total order defined by  S S , and  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( S ) \mathsf{transferSet}(S)  the set of  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  invocations contained in  S S  ( i.e. ,  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( S ) = { k ∈ [ 1 , | S | ] ∣ o k = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) } \mathsf{transferSet}(S)=\{k\in[1,|S|]\mid o_{k}=\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}(\star,\star)\} ). 
 
 •  
 
 A local history of a correct process  p i p_{i} , denoted  L i L_{i} , is a sequence of operation invocations made by  p i p_{i} .
If an invocation  o o  precedes another invocation  o ′ o^{\prime}  in  L i L_{i} , we say that “ o o  precedes  o ′ o^{\prime}  in the process order of  p i p_{i} ”, which is written  o → i o ′ o\rightarrow_{i}o^{\prime} . 
 
 •  
 
 In a similar way, a local history of a Byzantine process  p i p_{i}  is a sequence  L i L_{i}  of operations invocations. However, because Byzantine processes might deviate arbitrarily from their prescribed behavior, these invocations do not necessarily correspond to how the behavior of Byzantine processes is  perceived  by correct processes. 
 
 •  
 
 A global history  H H  is an array of  n n  local histories, one for each process:
 H = [ L 1 , … , L n ] H=[L_{1},...,L_{n}] . 
 
 •  
 
 We use the notion of  mock history  to capture the perceived behavior of Byzantine processes  [ 1 ] . Intuitively, a mock history  H ^ \widehat{H}  of some global history  H H  is a history that preserves the local histories  L i L_{i}  of correct processes  p i p_{i}  but might change the local histories of Byzantine processes. More formally,  H ^ = [ L ^ 1 , … , L ^ n ] \widehat{H}=[\widehat{L}_{1},...,\widehat{L}_{n}]  is a mock history of  H = [ L 1 , … , L n ] H=[L_{1},...,L_{n}]  iff  L ^ i = L i \widehat{L}_{i}=L_{i}  for all correct processes. 
 
 
 
 
 Active processes.   
Given a global history  H H , we say that a process is  active  in  H H  if it is involved in a  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j)  operation invocation appearing in a local history  L i L_{i}  of  H H , either as the calling process  p i p_{i}  or as the receiver  p j p_{j} . 
 
 
 AT-sequence.   
Given a process ID  i i  and a set of operation invocations  O O  (performed by  p i p_{i}  and other processes), the function  𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( i , O ) \mathit{total}(i,O)  returns the balance of the account of  p i p_{i}  resulting from the transfers it sends and receives according to  O O  (by adding the initial balance of  p i p_{i}  to the funds received by  p i p_{i}  in  O O , and subtracting the funds sent by  p i p_{i}  in  O O ): 
 

 
 
 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( i , O ) = 𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i + ( Σ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ​ ( v , i ) ∈ O ​ v ) − ( Σ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , ⋆ ) ∈ O ​ v ) . \mathit{total}(i,O)=\mathtt{init}_{i}+\big(\Sigma_{\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}(v,i)\in O}\,v\big)-\big(\Sigma_{\mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,\star)\in O}\,v\big). 
 
 
 
 
 A sequence  S S  of invocations is an  asset-transfer-sequence  ( AT-sequence ) if and only if: 
 
 1.  
 
 ∀ i ∈ [ 0 . . n ] , ∀ o = 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ( ) / v ∈ S : v = 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ( i , { o ′ ∈ S ∣ o ′ → S o } ) \Forall i\hskip 8.00003pt\in[0..n],\Forall o=\mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v\hskip 5.0pt\in S:v=\mathit{total}\big(i,\{o^{\prime}\in S\mid o^{\prime}\rightarrow_{S}o\}\big) ; 
 
 2.  
 
 ∀ i , j ∈ [ 0 . . n ] , ∀ o = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ( v , j ) ∈ S : v ≤ 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ( i , { o ′ ∈ S ∣ o ′ → S o } ) . \begin{aligned} \Forall i,j\in[0..n],\Forall o=\mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j)&\in S:v\leq\mathit{total}\big(i,\{o^{\prime}\in S\mid o^{\prime}\rightarrow_{S}o\}\big).\end{aligned} 
 
 
 Informally, these conditions mean that the  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  operation must return the balance of the process’s account when it is invoked in the sequence, and the  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  operation must succeed ( i.e. , return  𝚌𝚘𝚖𝚖𝚒𝚝 \mathtt{commit} ) only if the balance of the debtor’s account is sufficient according to the sequence  S S . 
 
 
 We say that a global history  H H  can be  AT-sequenced  iff for every correct process  p i p_{i}  there exists an  AT-sequence   S i S_{i}  with the following properties: 
 
 •  
 
 S i S_{i}  contains exactly all of  p i p_{i} ’s invocations (both  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i \mathsf{transfer}_{i}  and  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i \mathsf{balance}_{i}  invocations), and all transfers by other processes,  i.e. ,  𝗌𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( S i ) = 𝗌𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( L i ) ∪ ⋃ j ≠ i 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( L j ) . \mathsf{set}(S_{i})=\mathsf{set}(L_{i})\cup\bigcup_{j\neq i}\mathsf{transferSet}(L_{j}). 
 
 •  
 
 S i S_{i}  respects  p i p_{i} ’s process order,  i.e. ,  → i ⊆ → S i \rightarrow_{i}\subseteq\rightarrow_{S_{i}} . 
 
 
 Intuitively, the above conditions ensure that each process  p i p_{i}  can explain its local execution (Condition  1  of the AT-sequence definition above) from the transfers contained in the system in a way that respects  p i p_{i} ’s local order and the invariant that no account should ever be negative (Condition  2  of the AT-sequence definition). 
 
 
 AT properties.   
A distributed algorithm  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  implements the AT specification iff it provides  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  and  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  operations as defined earlier, such that the following properties hold. 
 
 •  
 
 AT-Termination.  All operation invocations of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  ( 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  and  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer} ) by correct processes terminate. 
 
 •  
 
 AT-Sequentiality.  For any global history  H H  capturing an execution of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , there exists a mock history  H ^ \widehat{H}  of  H H  that can be AT-sequenced. 
 
 
 
 
 Quasi-Anonymous Asset Transfer.   
An algorithm  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  implements a Quasi-Anonymous AT object (QAAT for short) if it verifies the AT properties (stated above) and also meets the following privacy-preserving properties.
Recall that  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  denotes an adversary trying to guess private information from system asset transfers. 
 
 •  
 
 QAAT-Receiver-Anonymity. 
For any global history  H H  capturing an execution of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , for any invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( ⋆ , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(\star,j)  contained in  H H  where  p i p_{i}  and  p j p_{j}  are correct processes, then, with high probability,  p j p_{j}  is indistinguishable among all the correct active processes of  H H . 
 
 •  
 
 QAAT-Confidentiality. 
For any global history  H H  capturing an execution of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , for any invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j)  contained in  H H  where  p i p_{i}  and  p j p_{j}  are correct processes, then, with high probability,  v v  is indistinguishable among any value in  ℝ + \mathbb{R}^{+} . 
 
 
 
 
 Informally, if we consider an adversary  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  and a  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j)  invocation appearing in a global history  H H , where  p i p_{i}  and  p j p_{j}  are correct processes, the best  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  can do (w.h.p.) is to pick  v ∈ ℝ + v\in\mathbb{R}^{+}  and  j ∈ [ 1 . . n ] j\in[1..n]  uniformly at random.
Doing so,  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  will guess  j j  correctly with a probability inversely proportional to the number of active processes in  H H , and  v v  with a probability of  0 0 . 7 7 
             7 
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          For simplicity, we assume that  v v  is defined on  ℝ + \mathbb{R}^{+} , but in practice,  v v  is a bounded positive number.
 
The above two properties capture that a Quasi-Anonymous Asset Transfer can be made arbitrarily close to this ideal situation by increasing the number of correct active processes and the security parameter  λ \lambda  of the cryptographic schemes.
Furthermore, in our QAAT implementation ( Algorithms   1 – 3 ), since each correct process  p i p_{i}  is the only one knowing its initial account balance  𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i \mathtt{init}_{i} , QAAT-Confidentiality ensures that the account balance of  p i p_{i}  also remains private throughout the execution with high probability. 
 
 
 
 5  A Modular Approach: Underlying Building Blocks 

 
 The QAAT algorithm proposed in this paper follows a modular approach.
Before moving to our actual contributions in  Section   6 , we present in this section the cryptographic schemes upon which our QAAT system builds, namely,  Commitments ,  Universal Accumulators  ( UA ), and  Zero-Knowledge Proofs  ( ZKP ).
Each scheme features a proving algorithm that aims to convince a verifying algorithm of some claim through a proof.
In the following, the proving algorithm is called the prover, while the verifying algorithm is called the verifier.
Due to space constraints, the full definition of the properties of these cryptographic schemes is given in  Appendix   A . 
 
 
 Commitments.   
A  commitment  scheme allows one to create a commitment  c c  to a chosen value  v v  while keeping this value hidden from others, with the ability to reveal the committed value later through a proof  o o  called opening. 
 
 
 A commitment scheme provides 2 operations:  (i)   𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝖼𝗈𝗆𝗆𝗂𝗍 ​ ( v ) / ⟨ c , o ⟩ \mathsf{c\_commit}(v)/\langle c,o\rangle  takes a value  v v  and outputs the corresponding commitment  c c  and opening  o o , and  (ii)   𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( c , v , o ) / r \mathsf{c\_verify}(c,v,o)/r  outputs  r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 r=\mathtt{true}  if  o o  is a valid opening for the commitment  c c  and value  v v , and  r = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 r=\mathtt{false}  otherwise.
Informally, a commitment scheme must be  binding  (it opens only to the committed value  v v ) and  hiding  (it does not leak information on its committed value). 
 
 
 Universal Accumulators (UA).   
A  universal accumulator  ( UA ) scheme  [ 7 ]  produces a short commitment  A A  (the accumulator) to a set of elements  E E , upon which the prover can produce both  membership proofs   w w  or  non-membership proofs   u u  of elements  e e  in the set  E E , without divulging any other information.
We assume each process creates an empty accumulator during the system’s setup phase (see  Appendix   E ). 
 
 
 Consider an accumulator  A A , its associated accumulated set  E E , and an arbitrary value  v v .
A UA scheme provides 6 operations:  (i)   𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗂𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝖾𝗆𝗉𝗍𝗒 ​ ( A ) / b \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_is\_empty}$}(A)/b  takes  A A  and outputs  b = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 b=\mathtt{true}  if  E = ∅ E=\varnothing , and  b = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 b=\mathtt{false}  otherwise;  (ii)   𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝖺𝖽𝖽 ​ ( A , v ) / A ′ \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_add}$}(A,v)/A^{\prime}  takes  A A  and  v v  and outputs the updated accumulator  A ′ A^{\prime}  containing  v v ;  (iii)   𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_mem}$}(A,E,v)/r  takes  A A ,  E E , and  v v  and outputs a membership proof  r = w r=w  of value  v v  in accumulator  A A  if  v ∈ E v\in E , and  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise;  (iv)   𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_non\_mem}$}(A,E,v)/r  takes  A A ,  E E , and  v v  and outputs a non-membership proof  r = u r=u  of value  v v  in  A A  if  v ∉ E v\notin E , and  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise;  (v)   𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , w ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_mem}$}(A,v,w)/r  outputs  r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 r=\mathtt{true}  if  w w  is a correct membership proof of  v v  for  A A , and  r = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 r=\mathtt{false}  otherwise; and  (vi)   𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , u ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_non\_mem}$}(A,v,u)/r  outputs  r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 r=\mathtt{true}  if  u u  is a correct non-membership proof of  v v  for  A A , and  r = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 r=\mathtt{false}  otherwise. 
 
 
 Informally, a UA scheme must be  sound  (proofs are unforgeable),  complete  (the verification of  (non-)membership  works as intended),  undeniable  (an element cannot have both membership and non-membership proofs), and  indistinguishable  (the accumulator and proofs do not leak information on the set). 
 
 
 Zero-Knowledge Proofs (ZKP).   
A  zero-knowledge proof  ( ZKP ) scheme  [ 42 ]  produces proofs  π \pi  that a prover knows some  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  without divulging any other information on it to the verifier.
Each object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}  of a ZKP scheme is set up with a specific predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} , called a  ZKP predicate , taking as parameters  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  (known both by the prover and verifier) and  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  (known only by the prover), and returning  𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathtt{true}  or  𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathtt{false} .
The prover  p i p_{i}  passes to the  𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{zkp\_prove}  operation the  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  and  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  parameters of  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} , and the proof  π i \pi_{i}  is correctly generated only if  P 𝑍𝐾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 CLOSE \mathit{P_{ZK}}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data} OPEN ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 )=\mathtt{true} .
The  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  predicate is typically passed to the implicit setup operation of the ZKP scheme. 
 
 
 Consider a ZKP object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}  set up with a ZKP predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} .
A ZKP scheme provides 2 operations:  (i)   𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_prove}$}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data})/r  outputs a result  r r , which can be either a zero-knowledge proof  r = π r=\pi  if the input parameters  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  and  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  satisfy  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  or  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise, and  (ii)   𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( π , 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_verify}$}(\pi,\mathit{public\_data})/r  outputs the validity  r ∈ { 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 , 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 } r\in\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}  of a zero-knowledge proof  π \pi  w.r.t. the  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  and the ZKP predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} .
Informally, a ZKP scheme must be  knowledge-sound  (if a proof  π \pi  is valid, then the prover knows the associated  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data} ),  complete  (a valid proof  π \pi  can be generated from some  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  and  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  that satisfy  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} ), and  zero-knowledge  (a proof  π \pi  does not leak information on  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data} ). 
 
 
 
 6  A Light Consensus-Free Quasi-Anonymous Asset Transfer Algorithm 

 
 Our novel asset-transfer system leverages the building blocks presented in  Section   5  and comprises two main contributions. The first, Agreement Proofs, presented in  Section   6.1  lies in a novel construct that produces transferable proofs that the processes have reached an agreement on some payload. The second, presented in  Section   6.2  consists of our novel asset-transfer algorithm, which leverages the building blocks presented in  section   5 , and our novel Agreement Proofs to guarantee  quasi-anonymity ,  lightness , and  consensus freedom . 
 
 
 6.1  A new distributed scheme: Agreement Proofs (AP) 

 
 An  Agreement proof  scheme ( AP ) is a novel distributed scheme defined by two (explicit) operations  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  and  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 \mathsf{ap\_verify} .
It aims at producing transferable agreement proofs (APs) that the system has reached an agreement regarding some payload value  v v  originating from a given sender/prover  p i p_{i}  with sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i} .
Sequence numbers uniquely identify each proof the prover generates using  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove} , making the scheme  multi-shot  ( i.e. , a given prover can generate multiple proofs).
Our asset-transfer algorithm, presented in  Section   6.2 , leverages APs to prevent double-spending.
Hence, the AP scheme, specified in the following, provides easily interpretable properties that formalize the notion of cryptographic certificates, which are often used in distributed systems  [ 3 ] .
In  Appendix   D , we provide a consensus-free AP implementation with a storage complexity of  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  and a communication complexity of  O ⁡ ( n ​ λ ) O(n\lambda) . 
 
 
 AP predicate.   
Each object of an AP scheme is set up with a specific predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}} , called an  AP predicate , taking in the AP’s sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  and some arbitrary  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  as parameters, and returning  𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathtt{true}  or  𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathtt{false} .
The prover  p i p_{i}  passes the payload  v v  and the  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  parameter of  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  to the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  operation, which generates a correct proof  σ i \sigma_{i}  only if  P A ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(v,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})=\mathtt{true} , where  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  is the sequence number of the current  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  invocation by  p i p_{i} ,  i.e. , the number of times the prover  p i p_{i}  has invoked  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  up to the current invocation.
We further assume that during system initialization, a valid  genesis AP  can be generated by the set-up procedure for a pair  ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) (v,\mathit{data})  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i = 0 \mathit{sn}_{i}=0 . (We return to the set-up procedure in  Section   6.2.1  and discuss implementation details in  Appendix   E .) 
 
 
 AP operations.   
We consider an AP object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A}  set up with an AP predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}} . 
 
 •  
 
 𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\mathit{data})/r : Given the prover  p i p_{i} , a payload value  v v  and some  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data} , the operation returns  r = σ i r=\sigma_{i}  if the predicate  P A ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) \mathit{P_{A}}(v,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})  is  𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathtt{true} , where  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  is the sequence number of the current  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  invocation by  p i p_{i} , and  σ i \sigma_{i}  is an agreement proof for value  v v  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i} , or  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise. 
 
 •  
 
 𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ j , v , 𝑠𝑛 j , j ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{j},v,\mathit{sn}_{j},j)/r : The operation returns the validity  r ∈ { 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 , 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 } r\in\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}  of an agreement proof  σ j \sigma_{j}  for a value  v v  of a prover  p j p_{j}  at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 j \mathit{sn}_{j} . 
 
 
 
 
 Validity of an AP  σ \bm{\sigma} .   
Given an AP object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} , a payload value  v v , a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  and a prover  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty), we say that some  σ \sigma  is a “ valid AP for  v v  at  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from  p i p_{i} ” if and only if any invocation of  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ , v , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ) \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma,v,\mathit{sn}_{i},i)  by any correct process would return true. 
 
 
 AP properties.   
An AP object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A}  set up with an AP predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  satisfies four properties. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 AP-Validity.  If  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for a value  v v  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i > 0 \mathit{sn}_{i}>0  from a correct prover  p i p_{i} , then  p i p_{i}  has executed  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( v , ⋆ ) / σ i \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\star)/\sigma_{i}  as its  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i} th  invocation of  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(\star,\star) . 
 
 •  
 
 AP-Agreement. 
There are no two different valid APs  σ i \sigma_{i}  and  σ i ′ \sigma_{i}^{\prime}  for two different values  v v  and  v ′ v^{\prime}  at the same sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  and from the same prover  p i p_{i} .
More formally,  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ i , v , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ) = 𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ i ′ , v ′ , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{i},v,\mathit{sn}_{i},i)=\mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{i}^{\prime},v^{\prime},\mathit{sn}_{i},i)=\mathtt{true}  implies  v = v ′ v=v^{\prime} . 
 
 •  
 
 AP-Knowledge-Soundness. 
If  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for value  v v  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i > 0 \mathit{sn}_{i}>0  from a prover  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty), then
 p i p_{i}  knows some  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  such that  P A ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) / 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(v,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})/\mathtt{true} . 
 
 •  
 
 AP-Termination. 
Given a correct process  p i p_{i}  that executes  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\mathit{data})/r  with value  v v  and a  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data} , if  P A ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(v,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})=\mathtt{true}  where  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  is the sequence number of the current  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  invocation by  p i p_{i} , then  r = σ i r=\sigma_{i}  where  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for  v v  at  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from  p i p_{i} .
If  P A ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(v,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})=\mathtt{false}  then  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.2  QAAT Algorithm 

 
 Our quasi-anonymous asset-transfer (QAAT) algorithm leverages agreement proofs to guarantee that there can be at most one transfer per sequence number from any process  p i p_{i}  ensuring that a process cannot spend the same funds twice. In addition, it leverages non-membership proofs to guarantee that a given transfer is not already in the receiver’s accumulator, ensuring that a process cannot redeem the same transfer twice. 
 
 
 6.2.1  Setup and initialization of process variables 

 
 We present in  Algorithm   1  the initialization of each of the variables maintained by each process  p i p_{i}  in our system:  𝑏𝑎𝑙 i \mathit{bal}_{i}  (the balance of process  p i p_{i} , only known to  p i p_{i}  and initialized to  𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i \mathtt{init}_{i} ),  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  (the current sequence number of  p i p_{i} ,  i.e. , the one for  p i p_{i} ’s latest transfer, initialized to 0),  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i \mathit{bal\_c}_{i}  and  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o i \mathit{bal\_o}_{i}  (respectively the commitment and opening of  𝑏𝑎𝑙 i = 𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i \mathit{bal}_{i}=\mathtt{init}_{i} ),  T i T_{i}  (the set of transfers details of  p i p_{i} , including debits and credits, initially empty),  A i A_{i}  (a universal accumulator of  T i T_{i} , recording all  p i p_{i} ’s debits and credits and initialized to the empty accumulator), and  σ i \sigma_{i}  (the previous agreement proof of  p i p_{i} , initialized to a valid AP for the initial accumulator and balance commitment of  p i p_{i} ).
We assume that the  𝑏𝑎𝑙 i \mathit{bal}_{i}  and  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  variables are of constant size ( e.g. , 64 bits). 
 
 
 
 
                 1 
               init:   𝑏𝑎𝑙 i ← 𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i \mathit{bal}_{i}\leftarrow\mathtt{init}_{i} ;
 𝑠𝑛 i ← 0 \mathit{sn}_{i}\leftarrow 0 ;
 ⟨ 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o i ⟩ ← 𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝖼𝗈𝗆𝗆𝗂𝗍 ​ ( 𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i ) \langle\mathit{bal\_c}_{i},\mathit{bal\_o}_{i}\rangle\leftarrow\mathsf{c\_commit}(\mathtt{init}_{i}) ;
 T i ← ∅ T_{i}\leftarrow\varnothing ;

 
     A i A_{i}   ← \leftarrow  empty universal accumulator of  p i p_{i} ; 
 
     σ i \sigma_{i}   ← \leftarrow  initial valid agreement proof for  ⟨ A i , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ⟩ \langle A_{i},\mathit{bal\_c}_{i}\rangle  at  𝑠𝑛 i = 0 \mathit{sn}_{i}=0  from  p i p_{i} .

 
 

 
 
 Algorithm 1   Initialization of the variables of  Algorithm   3  (code for  p i p_{i} ). 
 
 
 To initialize all these variables, and in particular  σ i \sigma_{i} , which involves communication among the processes, we employ the trustless setup procedure presented in  Appendix   E .

               if   𝑝𝑣𝑟 = 𝑠𝑛𝑑 \mathit{pvr}=\mathit{snd}   then   assert   𝑠𝑛 𝑠𝑛𝑑 = 𝑠𝑛 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ∧ 𝑏𝑎𝑙 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ≥ v \mathit{sn}_{\mathit{snd}}=\mathit{sn}_{\mathit{pvr}}\wedge\mathit{bal}_{\mathit{pvr}}\geq v ;
 
       
                 14 
               if   𝑝𝑣𝑟 = 𝑠𝑛𝑑 = 𝑟𝑐𝑣 \mathit{pvr}=\mathit{snd}=\mathit{rcv}   then   assert   𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 \mathsf{c\_verify} ( 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 𝑝𝑣𝑟 , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ ) (\mathit{bal\_c}^{\prime}_{\mathit{pvr}},\mathit{bal}_{\mathit{pvr}},\mathit{bal\_o}_{\mathit{pvr}}^{\prime}) ;
 
       
                 15 
               else if   𝑝𝑣𝑟 = 𝑠𝑛𝑑 \mathit{pvr}=\mathit{snd}   then   assert   𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 \mathsf{c\_verify} ( 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 𝑝𝑣𝑟 − v , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ ) ; (\mathit{bal\_c}^{\prime}_{\mathit{pvr}},\mathit{bal}_{\mathit{pvr}}-v,\mathit{bal\_o}^{\prime}_{\mathit{pvr}});\hskip-30.00005pt

               assert   𝐴𝑐𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡𝑈𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒 . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ 𝑠𝑛𝑑 , ⟨ A 𝑠𝑛𝑑 ′ , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑠𝑛𝑑 ⟩ CLOSE , \mathit{AccountUpdate}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{\mathit{snd}},\langle A^{\prime}_{\mathit{snd}},\mathit{bal\_c}_{\mathit{snd}}\rangle,

               else   𝗋𝖾𝗍𝗎𝗋𝗇 \mathsf{return}   𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathtt{false} .
 
 
 
 
 Algorithm 2   AP and ZKP predicates of  Algorithm   3 . 
 
 
 Our system relies on an AP object  𝐴𝑐𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡𝑈𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒 \mathit{AccountUpdate} , and a ZKP object  𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑉𝑎𝑙𝑖𝑑𝑖𝑡𝑦 \mathit{TransferValidity} , both shown in  Algorithm   2 . They are respectively set up with the AP predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  and the ZKP predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  on  algorithm   2 .
A process creates new AP and ZKP objects each time it sends or receives an asset transfer.
Hence, in these predicates, the prover can either be the sender or receiver of the asset transfer at hand.
We also assume that these predicates have access to the identity of the prover, denoted  𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{pvr} .
In the following,  𝑠𝑛𝑑 \mathit{snd}  and  𝑟𝑐𝑣 \mathit{rcv}  refer to the sender and receiver (resp.).
The statement  assert   B B , where  B B  evaluates to a Boolean, is a shortcut for “ if   ¬ B \neg B   then   𝗋𝖾𝗍𝗎𝗋𝗇 \mathsf{return}   𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathtt{false} ”.
Predicates return  𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathtt{true}  by default. 
 
 
 Predicate  P A {\bm{P_{A}}} .   
The  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  predicate takes 3 parameters: the payload  v v  (used both for the AP generation and verification), the  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  (used only for the AP generation), and the current sequence number of the prover  𝑠𝑛 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{sn}_{\mathit{pvr}} .
The  v v  parameter contains the new accumulator of the prover,  A 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ A^{\prime}_{\mathit{pvr}} , as well as the commitment to its new balance,  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ \mathit{bal\_c}^{\prime}_{\mathit{pvr}} .
The  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  parameter contains the previous AP of the prover,  σ 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \sigma_{\mathit{pvr}} , the current ZKP of the prover,  π 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \pi_{\mathit{pvr}} , and some data  𝑜𝑙𝑑 ​ _ ​ 𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑒 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{old\_state\_data}_{\mathit{pvr}}  about the state of the prover before the transfer.
This  𝑜𝑙𝑑 ​ _ ​ 𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑒 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{old\_state\_data}_{\mathit{pvr}}  contains the prover’s preceding accumulator  A 𝑝𝑣𝑟 A_{\mathit{pvr}}  and a commitment to its previous balance  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{bal\_c}_{\mathit{pvr}} . 
 
 
 P A \mathit{P_{A}}  starts with some unpacking ( algorithm   2 ), and tuple concatenation (operation  ⊕ \oplus ) to obtain the public data  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{public\_data}_{\mathit{pvr}}  describing the current transfer ( algorithm   2 ).  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{public\_data}_{\mathit{pvr}}  encompasses the prover’s accumulator before applying the transfer  A 𝑝𝑣𝑟 A_{\mathit{pvr}} , a commitment  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{bal\_c}_{\mathit{pvr}}  to the prover’s old balance, the prover’s accumulator after the transfer  A 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ A_{\mathit{pvr}}^{\prime} , and a commitment  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ \mathit{bal\_c}_{\mathit{pvr}}^{\prime}  to the prover’s new balance.
 P A \mathit{P_{A}}  then checks that the ZKP  π 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \pi_{\mathit{pvr}}  is valid ( algorithm   2 ) using the public data describing the transfer.
Then, if this is the first transfer of the prover (debit or credit),  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  checks that the prover’s preceding accumulator is empty ( algorithm   2 ).
Finally,  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  verifies the prover’s preceding AP ( algorithm   2 ). 
 
 
 Predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 {\bm{P_{\mathit{ZK}}}} .   
The  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  predicate takes two parameters as input:  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  and  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data} . The former,  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data} , consists of the tuple constructed at  algorithm   2 .
The latter,  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data} , consists of the sender’s accumulator  A 𝑠𝑛𝑑 A_{\mathit{snd}}  (recall that the sender is not always the prover), a commitment  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑠𝑛𝑑 \mathit{bal\_c}_{\mathit{snd}}  of the sender’s balance, the sender’s AP  σ 𝑠𝑛𝑑 \sigma_{\mathit{snd}} , the sender’s membership proof  w 𝑠𝑛𝑑 w_{\mathit{snd}}  that the transfer is in  A 𝑠𝑛𝑑 A_{\mathit{snd}}  (notice that all the previous parameters are equal to  ⊥ \bot  if the prover is not the receiver as in this case there are not used in the body of  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} ), the transfer details  τ \tau , the prover’s opening of the transfer  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{bal\_o}_{\mathit{pvr}} , the prover’s balance  𝑏𝑎𝑙 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{bal}_{\mathit{pvr}} , commitments of the prover’s balance before and after applying the transfer  𝑏𝑎𝑙 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{bal}_{\mathit{pvr}}  and  𝑏𝑎𝑙 𝑝𝑣𝑟 ′ \mathit{bal}^{\prime}_{\mathit{pvr}}  (resp.), and the prover’s non-membership proof  u 𝑝𝑣𝑟 u_{\mathit{pvr}}  that the transfer was not already in  A 𝑝𝑣𝑟 A_{\mathit{pvr}}  ( algorithm   2 ). 
 
 
 All the checks of  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  are done in zero-knowledge, without divulging any data to the verifier(s).
 P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  first checks that the commitments to the prover’s balance and to the prover’s transfer indeed open respectively to its balance ( algorithm   2 ).
 P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  then checks that the prover’s accumulator has been correctly updated,  i.e. , that the transfer did not already belong to the prover’s old accumulator, and that the prover’s new accumulator can be obtained by adding the transfer to its old accumulator ( algorithms   2  and  2 ).
Finally, the  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  predicate verifies two properties of the transfer:  (i)  that the prover’s balance has been updated according to the transfer’s information in  τ \tau  ( algorithms   2 – 2 ), and  (ii) , in case the prover is the receiver, that the passed AP  σ 𝑠𝑛𝑑 \sigma_{\mathit{snd}}  is valid and matches  τ \tau  on the sender’s side ( algorithms   2 – 2 ).
In more detail,  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  first verifies that if the prover is a sender, the transfer  τ \tau  is stamped with the same sequence number  𝑠𝑛 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{sn}_{\mathit{pvr}}  as that of the prover’s current AP ( algorithm   2 ,  𝑠𝑛 𝑝𝑣𝑟 \mathit{sn}_{\mathit{pvr}}  is passed as a parameter to  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  from  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  at  algorithm   2 ).
Then, if the prover, sender and receiver are the same,  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  checks that the prover’s balance remains unchanged ( algorithm   2 ) 8 8 
                 8 
                
                
                
                
                
                
                
              This condition is needed to support empty transfers, which are used to enhance the sender anonymity of the system.
 .
Otherwise, if the prover is only the sender,  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  ensures the transfer amount has been subtracted from the prover’s balance, and that the prover had enough funds to perform the transfer ( algorithm   2 ).
Finally, if the prover is only the receiver, then  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  verifies that the new prover’s balance was obtained by adding the transfer’s amount to its old balance ( algorithm   2 ), that the sender’s accumulator contains the transfer ( algorithm   2 ), and that the sender’s AP is valid ( algorithm   2 ).
If the prover is neither the sender nor the receiver of  τ \tau ,  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  returns  𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathtt{false}  ( algorithm   2 ). 
 
 
 
 6.2.3  Algorithm 

 
 Algorithm   3  presents the code of our QAAT implementation for a process  p i p_{i} .
The  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  operation simply returns the value of  𝑏𝑎𝑙 i \mathit{bal}_{i}  ( algorithm   3 ).
In the  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  operation,  p i p_{i}  first checks it has enough funds ( algorithm   3 ).
Then  p i p_{i}  creates the transfer details  τ \tau  with its next sequence number ( algorithm   3 ), processes its own transfer using the  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{process\_transfer}  internal operation ( algorithm   3 ), creates a membership proof  w i w_{i}  of the transfer in its accumulator ( algorithm   3 ), sends all the necessary information to the receiver in a  transfer  message ( algorithm   3 ) and returns  𝚌𝚘𝚖𝚖𝚒𝚝 \mathtt{commit}  ( algorithm   3 ).
When  p i p_{i}  receives a  transfer  message, it processes the transfer using the  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{process\_transfer}  internal operation if it is the transfer receiver, and if the sender’s AP and membership proof are valid ( algorithm   3 ).

               π ← 𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑉𝑎𝑙𝑖𝑑𝑖𝑡𝑦 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \pi\leftarrow\mathit{TransferValidity}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_prove}$}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data}) ;  
 
        ⊳ \rhd Obtaining Agreement Proof (AP) on transfer’s validity  
 
       
                 22 
               𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ← ⟨ σ i , π , 𝑜𝑙𝑑 ​ _ ​ 𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑒 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ⟩ \mathit{data}\leftarrow\langle\sigma_{i},\pi,\mathit{old\_state\_data}\rangle ; 

 
       
                 23 
               σ i ← 𝐴𝑐𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡𝑈𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒 . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( ⟨ A i ′ , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ′ ⟩ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \sigma_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{AccountUpdate}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(\langle A^{\prime}_{i},\mathit{bal\_c}^{\prime}_{i}\rangle,\mathit{data}) ;

                 24 
               𝑠𝑛 i ← 𝑠𝑛 i + 1 \mathit{sn}_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{sn}_{i}+1 ;  A i ← A i ′ A_{i}\leftarrow A^{\prime}_{i} ;  𝑏𝑎𝑙 i ← 𝑏𝑎𝑙 i ′ \mathit{bal}_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{bal}^{\prime}_{i} ;  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ← 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ′ \mathit{bal\_c}_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{bal\_c}^{\prime}_{i} ;
 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o i ← 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o i ′ \mathit{bal\_o}_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{bal\_o}^{\prime}_{i} . 


 
 

 
 
 Algorithm 3   QAAT algorithm (code for  p i p_{i} ). 
 
 
 In the  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{process\_transfer}  internal operation,  p i p_{i}  first
computes its new balance  𝑏𝑎𝑙 i ′ \mathit{bal}^{\prime}_{i}  depending on whether it is the sender or receiver ( algorithm   3 ),
and commits its new value ( algorithm   3 ).
Next,  p i p_{i}  proves the non-membership of the transfer  τ \tau  in its old accumulator  A i A_{i}  ( algorithm   3 ) and creates its new accumulator by adding the transfer ( algorithm   3 ).
Process  p i p_{i}  then constructs the ZK proof  π \pi  that the transfer  τ \tau  is valid
( algorithms   3 – 3 ).
It then creates the public data ( algorithm   3 ) and secret data ( algorithm   3 ) of the ZKP, and generates the ZKP ( algorithm   3 ).
Next,  p i p_{i}  creates the data of the Agreement Proof (AP) predicate ( algorithm   3 ).
Finally,  p i p_{i}  generates the AP  σ i \sigma_{i}  ( algorithm   3 ) before updating its local state ( algorithm   3 ). 
 
 
 
 6.2.4  Intuition of Algorithm  3 ’s proofs 

 
 In this section, we sketch the high-level intuition behind the correctness of our solution.
The full correctness developments are given in  Appendix   B . 
 
 
 Correctness proof.   
The correctness of our system comes from the fact that it satisfies AT-Sequentiality and AT-Termination. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.0   (AT-Sequentiality) . 
 
 For any global history  H H  capturing an execution of  Algorithm   3 , there exists a mock history  H ^ \widehat{H}  of  H H  that can be AT-sequenced. 
 
 
 
 Proof sketch. 
 
 The proof first constructs the mock history  H ^ \widehat{H}  by starting from the UAs linked to the APs generated by correct processes, and then recursively traversing these UAs and APs using the predicates  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  and  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  to uncover UAs issued by Byzantine processes that are causally linked to the operations of correct processes. Each UA yields information on a transfer invocation at a given sequence number, which we then use to construct the mock local execution  L ^ j \widehat{L}_{j}  of each Byzantine process  p j p_{j} . 
 
 
 Given a correct process  p i p_{i} , we then construct a partial  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  order on  𝒮 i = 𝗌𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( L ^ i ) ∪ ⋃ j ≠ i 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( L ^ j ) \mathcal{S}_{i}=\mathsf{set}(\widehat{L}_{i})\cup\bigcup_{j\neq i}\mathsf{transferSet}(\widehat{L}_{j}) . This construction is somewhat technical for two reasons: (1) Transfers might not be received in the order they were issued (in particular by  p i p_{i} ), which implies  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  should not enforce any local process order  → j \rightarrow_{j}  other than that of  p i p_{i} . (2) Simultaneously,  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  should be constraining enough to guarantee that  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  invocations by  p i p_{i}  exactly reflect previous transfers (Property n. 1  of an AT-sequence in  Section   4 ) and that each transfer is backed by sufficient funds (Property n. 2 ). We construct  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  incrementally using two binary relations:  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  that captures  p i p_{i} ’s local order and ensures that all transfers are sufficiently funded, and  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  to guarantee  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  invocations by  p i p_{i}  can be properly explained. Part of the proof focuses on the acyclicity of  ↝ 1 i ∪ ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\cup\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} , so that  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  can be defined as the transitive closure  ( ↝ 1 i ∪ ↝ 2 i ) + (\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\cup\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i})^{+} . Finally, we chose  S i S_{i}  as a topological sort of  ( 𝒮 i , ↝ i ) (\mathcal{S}_{i},\rightsquigarrow_{i})  and prove it fulfills the two properties of AT-Sequences.
(Full derivations in  Section   B.1 .)
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 6.0   (AT-Termination) . 
 
 All operation invocations of  Algorithm   3  ( 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  and  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer} ) terminate for correct processes. (Proof in  Section   B.1 .) 
 
 
 
 Quasi-anonymity proof.   
Intuitively, our system is quasi-anonymous because it uses cryptographic schemes that do not leak sensitive data ( i.e. , commitments, universal accumulators, and zero-knowledge proofs).
We prove the following lemmas in  Section   B.2 . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.0   (QAAT-Receiver-Anonymity) . 
 
 For any global history  H H  capturing an execution of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , for any invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( ⋆ , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(\star,j)  contained in  H H  where  p i p_{i}  and  p j p_{j}  are correct processes, then, with high probability,  p j p_{j}  is indistinguishable among all the correct active processes of  H H .
(Proof in  Section   B.2 .) 
 
 
 
 Lemma 6.0   (QAAT-Confidentiality) . 
 
 For any global history  H H  capturing an execution of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , for any invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(v,j)  contained in  H H  where  p i p_{i}  and  p j p_{j}  are correct processes, then, with high probability,  v v  is indistinguishable among any value in  ℝ + \mathbb{R}^{+} .
(Proof in  Section   B.2 .) 
 
 
 
 Succinctness.   
The overall succinctness of our system stems from the succinctness of our Agreement Proof implementation ( Section   D.5 ), the constant size of the digests and proofs of RSA accumulators ( Section   C.2 ) and of commitments ( Section   C.3 ), and of the succinctness of Spartan zk-SNARKs ( Section   C.4 ).
By definition, the proofs of computation produced by the prover of a succinct ZKP scheme are succinctly verified. Therefore, primitives whose verification is encapsulated inside the ZKP predicate only need to be constant size for our scheme to be succinct (although accumulator (non-)membership proof verification and commitment openings can, in fact, be succinct). 
 
 
 Storage of  O ⁡ ( λ + ( | T | / n ) ​ 𝐥𝐨𝐠 ⁡ n + n ) \bm{O(\lambda+(|T|/n)\log n+n)}  bits per correct process.   
In our system, each correct process  p i p_{i}  only stores its local transfer details, the sequence number of each of the  n n  processes, and a few cryptographic structures, yielding a storage of  O ⁡ ( λ + ( | T | / n ) ​ log ⁡ n + n ) O(\lambda+(|T|/n)\log n+n) , where  T T  is the set of all transfers in the system.
Let us consider the size of  p i p_{i} ’s local variables.
By definition,  𝑏𝑎𝑙 i \mathit{bal}_{i}  and  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  are constant-size (see  Section   6.2.3 ).
Moreover,  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i \mathit{bal\_c}_{i}  and  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o i \mathit{bal\_o}_{i}  have  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits with a constant size commitment scheme ( Section   C.3 ),  A i A_{i}  has  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits with the constant size RSA accumulator implementation ( Section   C.2 ), and  σ i \sigma_{i}  has  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits with our implementation of agreement proofs ( Appendix   D ).
Moreover, our AP algorithm stores  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits per correct process ( Appendix   D ).
Finally, the  T i T_{i}  set is of size  O ⁡ ( | T | / n ) O(|T|/n)  (where  T T  is the set of all transfers of the system) and contains transfer details of size  O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n)  bits (because of process IDs).
This amounts to a total storage cost of  O ⁡ ( λ + ( | T | / n ) ​ log ⁡ n + n ) O(\lambda+(|T|/n)\log n+n) . 
 
 
 Communication of  O ⁡ ( n ​ λ ) \bm{O(n\lambda)}  bits overall.   
In our system, a  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( ⋆ , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(\star,j)  invocation by a correct process  p i p_{i}  entails the following communications:  (i)  the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  invocation by the sender  p i p_{i}  at  algorithm   3 ,  (ii)  the  𝗋𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{ra\_send}  of the transfer details from the sender to the receiver at  algorithm   3 , and  (iii)  the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  invocation by the receiver  p j p_{j}  at  algorithm   3  (which is only guaranteed to happen if  p j p_{j}  is correct).
As our Agreement Proof implementation ( Appendix   D ) communicates only  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n)  bits overall ( Section   D.7 ),  (i)  and  (iii)  incur an overall communication of  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n) .
For  (ii) , the receiver-anonymous  𝗋𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{ra\_send}  operation can be implemented with an overall communication cost of  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n) , as discussed in  Section   3 .
As a result, our system’s overall communication cost is  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n) . 
 
 
 Consensus-freedom.   
This directly comes from our distributed Agreement Proof implementation ( Appendix   D ), which does not rely on strong agreement such as consensus ( Section   D.4 ). 
 
 
 
 6.2.5  Further enhancements 

 
 Transfer batching.   
One could argue that requiring users to commit an accumulator update to the system each time they send or receive a single transfer is inefficient. To address this problem, we propose aggregating an arbitrary number of transfers into a single accumulator update.
As a result, a user could choose only to declare an accumulator update when making a payment, while cashing all receipts simultaneously when doing so.
To implement transfer batching, we use an optimization method for ZK proofs known as  folding   [ 32 ]  in  Appendix   F . 
 
 
 Reducing local storage (public key rotation).   
The QAAT system presented in this article is light,  i.e. , the storage cost per process  p i p_{i}  is in  O ⁡ ( λ + ( | T | / n ) ​ log ⁡ n + n ) O(\lambda+(|T|/n)\log n+n) , where  | T | |T|  is the set of all transfers (debits and credits) of the system.
This storage cost is justified by  p i p_{i} ’s need to store some data whose size is proportional to its entire transfer history, to prove that it is not trying to redeem the same transfer several times.
However, a public key rotation mechanism could be added to remove the need to store past transfers.
When a process  p i p_{i}  rotates its public key, it changes its old public key for a new one, while transferring all its funds to the account associated to the new public key.
Once this is done,  p i p_{i}  can flush its old local data (and, in particular, its accumulator data).
Thus, the process only has to record information concerning this rotating transfer which can be seen as the genesis state of a newly created account, and obtains a storage cost of  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n) .
However, this mechanism would involve one major technical challenge: a sender has to retrieve the receiver’s public key before it can send funds to her.
To achieve this retrieval, the sender can initiate a handshake with the receiver, but due to asynchrony and the fact that the receiver may be faulty, the handshake may never complete, hampering the termination of the transfer.
This method is briefly mentioned by Zef  [ 3 ]  as a solution to safely delete the data of unused accounts. To circumvent the incompatibility of its asynchronous model and the handshake, Zef assumes that each transfer is initiated in a synchronous environment outside the system where the receiver transmits its public key to the sender. 
 
 
 Towards full anonymity.    Our system is not fully anonymous, as it guarantees Receiver Anonymity and Confidentiality, but not that the transfer issuer remains anonymous ( i.e. , Sender Anonymity).
However, remark that our system allows “empty” transfers with amount 0 (or transfers to oneself), which do not change any balance.
These empty transfers could be used to obfuscate the traffic of funds from the adversary’s point of view, and if they are issued at the right moment, we conjecture that they could preclude (w.h.p.) timing attacks,  i.e. , attacks where an adversary deanonymizes the sender (or receiver) of an asset transfer by observing the timing of messages transiting on the network.
If so, our system would reach full anonymity asymptotically (by adding more empty transfers at some well-chosen moments).
The design of the heuristics to choose the moments to issue empty transfers is left to future work. 
 
 
 
 
 
 7  Conclusion 

 
 This article considered the problem of asynchronous Byzantine-fault-tolerant asset transfer, with the additional constraint of satisfying the properties of quasi-anonymity ( i.e. , no leak of information on the transfers’ amounts and receivers), lightness ( i.e. , succinct cryptography and light storage cost), and consensus-freedom ( i.e. , no total order of transfers).
These properties are important for achieving good performance, confidentiality, and user privacy in an asset transfer system.
In this context, this article introduced Quasi-Anonymous Asset Transfer (QAAT), and presented a consensus-free and light QAAT implementation, along with its formal proofs.
To our knowledge, our asset transfer system is not only the first to fulfill the 3 properties, but also the first one to have a  O ⁡ ( λ ​ | T | / n ) O(\lambda|T|/n)  storage cost, where  T T  is the set of all transfers of the system.
In addition, the article presented a new distributed abstraction called Agreement Proofs, which captures the notion of distributed agreement in a transferable short-sized proof. 
 
 
 Presently, our asset transfer solution still lacks some capabilities compared to more mature blockchain systems ( e.g. , Sybil resistance or smart contracts) or mainstream payment networks ( e.g. , overdrawn accounts or fraud detection), but we believe that it demonstrates that systems with low verification, storage, and network costs can still guarantee strong privacy features.
Furthermore, we conjecture that our system’s scalability can be further enhanced, and in particular, that it can be made permissionless without sacrificing consensus-freedom, by leveraging techniques such as the ones introduced in  [ 33 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix A  Underlying Building Blocks: Full Definitions 

 
 This appendix section presents the full definitions of the cryptographic schemes used in our QAAT system, namely Commitments, Universal Accumulators (UA), and Zero-Knowledge Proofs (ZKP).
More precisely, this section provides the operations and properties of these schemes. 
 
 
 These schemes all require an implicit  setup  operation that takes as input the security parameter  λ \lambda  and outputs the public parameters of the scheme, which are known to all processes, including the adversary.
For instance, in a digital signature scheme, public parameters correspond to the set of all the system’s public keys.
All the cryptographic schemes that follow implicitly use some public parameters (although these parameters are not exposed explicitly in the specifications and algorithms).
The domains of definition for the input values and output proofs of our schemes are respectively called  𝔻 \mathbb{D}  and  𝔽 \mathbb{F}  (see  Table   3 ).
Each scheme features a proving algorithm that aims to convince a verifying algorithm of some claim through a proof.
In the following, the proving algorithm is called the prover, while the verifying algorithm is called the verifier.
Formally, both the prover and the verifier are poly-time probabilistic algorithms.
For simplicity, the schemes presented in this section do not have explicit termination properties, as their operations only consist of local mathematical computations. 
 
 
 We further denote by  ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) \epsilon(\lambda)  a positive number between  0 0  and  1 1  that can be made arbitrarily small by increasing  λ \lambda . 
 
 
 
 
 Set 
 Meaning 
 
 𝔻 \mathbb{D}  (for “data”) 
 Input set (values) of the proving operations of the cryptographic schemes 
 
 𝔽 \mathbb{F}  (for “finite”) 
 Output set (proofs) of the proving operations of the cryptographic schemes 
 
 Table 3:  Input and output sets of the cryptographic schemes of  Appendix   A . 
 
 
 A.1  Commitment schemes 

 
 A commitment scheme is a cryptographic primitive that enables a prover to succinctly commit to a value  v v  in the form a small digest  c c  (the commitment) and compute a proof  o o  (the opening) that the commitment “opens” to  v v .
Commitment schemes must be binding,  i.e. , the prover cannot find another value that matches the digest.
Most schemes also have a hiding property, meaning that the digest does not reveal any information about the committed value.
Some schemes allow digests to be combined to perform operations ( e.g. , addition or multiplication) on committed values, such as homomorphic schemes.
There are many variants of commitments to handle different types of data, such as polynomials, vectors, or even functions.
In our system, we use a simple  commitment  scheme ( C ) to a bounded integer. 
 
 
 Commitment operations.    
 
 •  
 
 𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝖼𝗈𝗆𝗆𝗂𝗍 ​ ( v ) / ⟨ c , o ⟩ \mathsf{c\_commit}(v)/\langle c,o\rangle  takes a value  v ∈ 𝔻 v\in\mathbb{D}  and outputs the corresponding commitment  c c  and opening  o o . 
 
 •  
 
 𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( c , v , o ) / r \mathsf{c\_verify}(c,v,o)/r  outputs  r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 r=\mathtt{true}  if  o o  is a valid opening for the commitment  c c  and value  v ∈ 𝔻 v\in\mathbb{D} , and  r = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 r=\mathtt{false}  otherwise. 
 
 
 
 
 Commitment properties.

 Informally, a commitment created from a value opens to this value (using an opening). 
 
 •  
 
 C-Binding. 
 ∀ ⁡ c , o , o ′ ∈ 𝔽 , ∀ ⁡ v , v ′ ∈ 𝔻 : Pr ⁡ ( 𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 CLOSE \Forall c,o,o^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F},\Forall v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}:\Pr\big(\mathsf{c\_verify} OPEN ( c , v , o ) / 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 ∧ 𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( c , v ′ , o ′ ) / 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 ∧ v ′ ≠ v ) < ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) .
