Informally, a commitment opens to only one value (w.h.p.). 
 
 •  
 
 C-Hiding. 
 ∀ ⁡ c 1 , c 2 ∈ 𝔽 , ∀ ⁡ v ∈ 𝔻 , 𝖼 ​ _ ​ 𝖼𝗈𝗆𝗆𝗂𝗍 ​ ( v ) / ⟨ c , ⋆ ⟩ : | Pr ⁡ ( c = c 1 ) − Pr ⁡ ( c = c 2 ) | < ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) \Forall c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{F},\Forall v\in\mathbb{D},\mathsf{c\_commit}(v)/\langle c,\star\rangle:|\Pr(c=c_{1})-\Pr(c=c_{2})|\!<\!\epsilon(\lambda) . 
 
 
 Informally, a commitment does not leak any information on its committed value (w.h.p). 
 
 
 
 
 
 A.2  Universal Accumulators (UA) 

 
 An  accumulator  scheme (notion introduced in  [ 4 ] ) produces a short commitment to a set of elements  E E .
A  universal accumulator  ( UA ) is a special kind of accumulator that can prove both the inclusion or non-inclusion of an element in the set by using  membership  or  non-membership proofs , respectively.
RSA accumulators  [ 7 ]  are a notable implementation of this scheme.
We assume that the empty accumulator of each process is created during the system’s setup phase (see  Appendix   E ). 
 
 
 UA operations.    We consider an accumulator  A A  and its associated accumulated set  E E . 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗂𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝖾𝗆𝗉𝗍𝗒 ​ ( A ) / b \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_is\_empty}$}(A)/b : Takes  A A  and outputs  b = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 b=\mathtt{true}  if  E = ∅ E=\varnothing , and  b = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 b=\mathtt{false}  otherwise. 
 
 •  
 
 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝖺𝖽𝖽 ​ ( A , v ) / A ′ \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_add}$}(A,v)/A^{\prime} : Takes  A A  and a value  v v  and outputs the updated accumulator  A ′ A^{\prime}  containing  v v . 
 
 •  
 
 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_mem}$}(A,E,v)/r : Takes  A A ,  E E , and a value  v v  and outputs a membership proof  r = w r=w  of value  v v  in accumulator  A A  if  v ∈ E v\in E , and  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise. 
 
 •  
 
 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_non\_mem}$}(A,E,v)/r : Takes  A A ,  E E , and a value  v v  and outputs a non-membership proof  r = u r=u  of value  v v  in  A A  if  v ∉ E v\notin E , and  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise. 
 
 •  
 
 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , w ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_mem}$}(A,v,w)/r : Outputs  r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 r=\mathtt{true}  if  w w  is a correct membership proof of value  v v  for  A A , and  r = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 r=\mathtt{false}  otherwise. 
 
 •  
 
 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , u ) / r \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_non\_mem}$}(A,v,u)/r : Outputs  r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 r=\mathtt{true}  if  u u  is a correct non-membership proof of value  v v  for  A A , and  r = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 r=\mathtt{false}  otherwise. 
 
 
 
 
 UA properties.    We consider an accumulator  A A  and its associated set  E E . 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 UA-Soundness.   ∀ ⁡ v ∉ E , 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) / r : Pr ⁡ ( r ≠ 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 ) < ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) \Forall v\notin E,\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_mem}$}(A,E,v)/r:\Pr(r\neq\mathtt{abort})<\epsilon(\lambda)  and
 ∀ ⁡ v ∈ E , 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) / r : Pr ⁡ ( r ≠ 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 ) < ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) \Forall v\in E,\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_non\_mem}$}(A,E,v)/r:\Pr(r\neq\mathtt{abort})<\epsilon(\lambda) . 
 
 
 Informally, the probability of computing a membership proof for a non-accumulated element or a non-membership proof for an accumulated element is negligible. 
 
 •  
 
 UA-Completeness.   ∀ ⁡ v ∈ E , 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) ) / r : Pr ⁡ ( r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 ) > 1 − ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) \Forall v\in E,\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_mem}$}(A,v,\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_mem}$}(A,E,v))/r:\Pr(r=\mathtt{true})>1-\epsilon(\lambda)  and
 ∀ ⁡ v ∉ E , 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , E , v ) ) / r : Pr ⁡ ( r = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 ) > 1 − ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) \Forall v\notin E,\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_non\_mem}$}(A,v,\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_prove\_non\_mem}$}(A,E,v))/r\!:\Pr(r=\mathtt{true})>1-\epsilon(\lambda) . 
 
 
 Informally, all honestly accumulated values are verified as true with their corresponding membership proof with a negligible probability of error. 
 
 •  
 
 UA-Undeniability.   ∀ ⁡ v ∈ 𝔻 \Forall v\in\mathbb{D} ,  ∀ ⁡ w , u ∈ 𝔽 : Pr ⁡ ( 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , w ) ∧ 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A , v , u ) ) < ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) \Forall w,u\in\mathbb{F}:\\
\Pr(\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_mem}$}(A,v,w)\land\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_non\_mem}$}(A,v,u))<\epsilon(\lambda) . 
 
 
 Informally, the probability of computing both a membership and non-membership proof for the same element is negligible. 
 
 •  
 
 UA-Indistinguishability.  No information on some accumulated set  E E  is leaked from its associated accumulator  A A , membership proofs  w w  or non-membership proofs  u u . 9 9 
                     9 
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                  For a more formal definition of Indistinguishability, we refer the interested reader to  [ 17 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 For simplicity and compliance with the traditional definitions given in the cryptography literature  [ 7 ] , we do not give properties for the  𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝖺𝖽𝖽 \mathsf{ua\_add}  operations.
We implicitly assume that they behave correctly, that is, we can only prove the membership of an element that has been added and we can only prove the non-membership for an element that has never been added. 
 
 
 
 A.3  Zero-Knowledge Proofs (ZKP) 

 
 We refer to the last scheme used in this article as  zero-knowledge proofs  ( ZKP ), which correspond to proofs that a prover knows some secret data without divulging any other information on it to the verifier.
Specifically, we use zero-knowledge Succinct Non-Interactive Arguments of Knowledge (zk-SNARKs)  [ 42 ] .
The difference between proof and argument systems comes from the strength of the soundness property.
A proof system must be perfectly sound ( i.e. , withstand a computationally unbounded adversary) whereas an argument system guarantees computational soundness ( i.e. , against a PPT adversary with a very high probability).
The use of arguments is necessary as it was proven by Fortnow  [ 20 ]  that no perfect soundness zero-knowledge proof systems exist for NP-complete problems, while there exist perfect zero-knowledge arguments systems for NP-complete problems ( e.g. , zk-SNARKs). 
 
 
 ZKP predicate.   
Each object of a ZKP scheme is set up with a specific predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} , called a  ZKP predicate , taking as parameters a  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  (known both by the prover and verifier) and a  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  (known only by the prover), and returning  𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathtt{true}  or  𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathtt{false} .
The prover  p i p_{i}  passes to the  𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{zkp\_prove}  operation the  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  and  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  parameters of  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} , and the proof  π i \pi_{i}  is correctly generated only if  P 𝑍𝐾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{ZK}}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data})=\mathtt{true} .
Much like the  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  predicate ( Section   6.1 ), the  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  predicate is typically passed to the implicit setup operation of the ZKP scheme. 
 
 
 ZKP operations.   
We consider a ZKP object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}  set up with a ZKP predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} . 
 
 •  
 
 𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_prove}$}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data})/r : Returns a result  r r , which can be either a zero-knowledge proof  r = π r=\pi  if the input parameters  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  and  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  satisfy  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} , or  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort}  otherwise. 
 
 •  
 
 𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( π , 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_verify}$}(\pi,\mathit{public\_data})/r : Returns the validity  r ∈ { 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 , 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 } r\in\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}  of a zero-knowledge proof  π \pi  with respect to the public data  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  and the ZKP predicate. 
 
 
 
 
 ZKP properties.   
We consider a ZKP object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}  set up with a ZKP predicate  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} . 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 ZKP-Knowledge-Soundness. 
If  𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( π , 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_verify}$}(\pi,\mathit{public\_data})  is true, then the prover knows some  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  such that  𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / π \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_prove}$}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data})/\pi  and  P 𝑍𝐾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \mathit{P_{ZK}}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data})  is true w.h.p. 
 
 •  
 
 ZKP-Completeness. 
For any pair  ⟨ 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ⟩ \langle\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data}\rangle  such that  P 𝑍𝐾 ​ ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \mathit{P_{ZK}}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data})  is true, we must have  𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ( 𝑂𝑏𝑗 𝑍𝐾 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ( 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 _ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 _ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) , 𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 _ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_verify}$}(\mathit{Obj}_{\mathit{ZK}}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_prove}$}(\mathit{public\_data},\mathit{secret\_data}),\mathit{public\_data})  is true w.h.p. 
 
 •  
 
 ZKP-Zero-Knowledge.  No information on some  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  is leaked by its associated  𝑝𝑢𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑐 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{public\_data}  and zero-knowledge proof  π \pi . 10 10 
                     10 
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                  For a more formal definition of Zero-Knowledge, we refer the interested reader to  [ 25 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix B  Proofs of our QAAT System (Algorithms  1  to  3 ) 

 
 In this appendix section, we provide full derivations on the proof of correctness ( Section   B.1 ) and quasi-anonymity ( Section   B.2 ) of our QAAT system ( Algorithms   1 – 3 ).
Throughout the section, we rely on the full property definitions of the commitment, UA, and ZKP schemes given in  Appendix   A . 
 
 
 B.1  Proof of correctness 

 
 B.1.1  Preliminaries 

 
 We first introduced a number of preliminary results and definitions that we will use to prove the AT-Sequentiality of  Algorithm   3  in  Section   B.1.2 .
In the following,  σ i 𝑠𝑛 \sigma_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  denotes an AP (Agreement Proof) that is valid at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for a process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty). 
 
 
 Definition B.1   (Valid UA at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for a process  p i p_{i} ) . 
 
 We say that a Universal Accumulator (UA)  A A  is valid at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for a process  p i p_{i}  (correct of faulty) if there exists a valid Agreement Proof (AP)  σ i 𝑠𝑛 \sigma_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  that is valid at  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for  p i p_{i}  and verifies  𝐴𝑐𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡𝑈𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒 . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ i 𝑠𝑛 , ⟨ A , ⋆ ⟩ , 𝑠𝑛 , i ) \mathit{AccountUpdate}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{i}^{\mathit{sn}},\langle A,\star\rangle,\mathit{sn},i) .
As a shortcut, we might interchangeably say that  A A  was  issued  by  p i p_{i}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn} .
When this holds, we note  A A ’s sequence number and AP issuer as superscript and subscript, respectively, i.e.,  A = A i 𝑠𝑛 A=A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}} . 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.2   (Unicity of valid UAs at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for a process  p i p_{i} ) . 
 
 There is at most one UA that is valid at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 ≥ 0 \mathit{sn}\geq 0  for a process  p i p_{i}  (correct of faulty), i.e., if  A A  and  A ′ A^{\prime}  are both valid UAs at  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for  p i p_{i}  according to  Definition   B.1 , then  A = A ′ A=A^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This trivially follows from  Definition   B.1  and the AP-Agreement property of Agreement Proofs.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.3   (Sequence of preceding UAs of a valid UA) . 
 
 Consider a valid UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 ≥ 0 \mathit{sn}\geq 0  issued by a process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty). The following holds. 
 
 •  
 
 A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  can be associated with a unique sequence of preceding UAs, denoted  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ) = ( A i 0 , A i 1 , . . , A i 𝑠𝑛 ) \mathit{precedingUAs}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}})=(A_{i}^{0},A_{i}^{1},..,A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}) , so that each  A i k A_{i}^{k}  is a valid UA issued by  p i p_{i}  at sequence number  k k , with  0 ≤ k ≤ 𝑠𝑛 0\leq k\leq\mathit{sn} . 
 
 •  
 
 The sequences of UAs produced for UAs issued by the same process  p i p_{i}  are prefix-ordered, i.e., if  A i 𝑠𝑛 1 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{1}}  and  A i 𝑠𝑛 2 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{2}}  are two valid UAs issued by  p i p_{i}  at sequence numbers  𝑠𝑛 1 \mathit{sn}_{1}  and  𝑠𝑛 2 \mathit{sn}_{2}  respectively such that  𝑠𝑛 1 ≤ 𝑠𝑛 2 \mathit{sn}_{1}\leq\mathit{sn}_{2} , then  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 1 ) \mathit{precedingUAs}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{1}})  is a prefix of  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 2 ) \mathit{precedingUAs}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{2}}) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let us consider a valid UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 ≥ 0 \mathit{sn}\geq 0  issued by a process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty). 
 
 •  
 
 If  𝑠𝑛 = 0 \mathit{sn}=0 , we pick  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( A i 0 ) = ( A i 0 ) \mathit{precedingUAs}(A_{i}^{0})=(A_{i}^{0}) . 
 
 •  
 
 If  𝑠𝑛 > 0 \mathit{sn}>0 , by  Definition   B.1 ,  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is associated with some AP  σ i 𝑠𝑛 \sigma_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  issued by  p i p_{i}  that is valid at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn} .
By AP-Knowledge-Soundness,  p i p_{i}  knows some  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 = ⟨ σ i ′ , ⋆ , ⋆ ⟩ \mathit{data}=\langle\sigma_{i}^{\prime},\star,\star\rangle  s.t.  P A ​ ( ⋆ , ⟨ σ i ′ , ⋆ , ⋆ ⟩ , 𝑠𝑛 ) \mathit{P_{A}}(\star,\langle\sigma_{i}^{\prime},\star,\star\rangle,\mathit{sn}) 
is true.  Algorithm   2  of the code of Predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  ( Algorithm   2 ) implies that  σ i ′ \sigma_{i}^{\prime}  is a valid AP at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 − 1 \mathit{sn}-1  from  p i p_{i}  for some payload  ⟨ A 𝑝𝑣𝑟 , ⋆ ⟩ \langle A_{\mathit{pvr}},\star\rangle .  A 𝑝𝑣𝑟 A_{\mathit{pvr}}  fulfills  Definition   B.1 , and is therefore a valid UA at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 − 1 \mathit{sn}-1  from  p i p_{i} .
By induction, we obtain that, for each  𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sn}^{\prime}  s.t.  0 ≤ 𝑠𝑛 ′ ≤ 𝑠𝑛 0\leq\mathit{sn}^{\prime}\leq\mathit{sn}  there is a valid UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 ′ A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}}  at  𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sn}^{\prime}  from  p i p_{i} . We denote this sequence as  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ( A i ′ 𝑠𝑛 ) = ( A i 0 , A i 1 , . . , A i 𝑠𝑛 ) \mathit{precedingUAs}({A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\mathit{sn}})=(A_{i}^{0},A_{i}^{1},..,A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}) . 
 
 
 By  Lemma   B.2 ,  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( A i ′ 𝑠𝑛 ) \mathit{precedingUAs}({A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\mathit{sn}})  is unique.
We say that the sequence of valid UAs  ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ′ ) 0 ≤ 𝑠𝑛 ′ ≤ 𝑠𝑛 (A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}})_{0\leq\mathit{sn}^{\prime}\leq\mathit{sn}}  is the  sequence of preceding UAs  of  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}} . Prefix ordering similarly follows from  Lemma   B.2 .∎ 
 
 
 
 Definition B.4   (Valid transfer  τ \tau  at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for a process  p i p_{i} ) . 
 
 We say that that a transfer  τ = ⟨ 𝑠𝑛𝑑 , v , 𝑟𝑐𝑣 , 𝑠𝑛 𝑠𝑛𝑑 ⟩ \tau=\langle\mathit{snd},v,\mathit{rcv},\mathit{sn}_{\mathit{snd}}\rangle  is valid at a sequence number  𝑠𝑛 > 0 \mathit{sn}>0  for a process  p i p_{i}  (correct of faulty) if there exist a non-membership proof  u u  and two valid UAs  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  and  A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1}  issued by  p i p_{i}  at sequence numbers  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  and  𝑠𝑛 − 1 \mathit{sn}-1  respectively, such that: 
 

 
 
 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ _ ​ 𝗇𝗈𝗇 ​ _ ​ 𝗆𝖾𝗆 ​ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 , τ , u ) ∧ 𝗎𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝖺𝖽𝖽 ​ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 , τ ) = A i 𝑠𝑛 . \textup{$\mathsf{ua\_verify\_non\_mem}$}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1},\tau,u)\land\textup{$\mathsf{ua\_add}$}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1},\tau)=A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}. 
 
 (1) 
 
 When this holds, we note  τ \tau ’s sequence number and UA issuer 11 11 
                     11 
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                   We note that the UA issuer of transfer  τ \tau  is not always the sender of  τ \tau .  as superscript and subscript, respectively, i.e.,  τ = τ i 𝑠𝑛 \tau=\tau_{i}^{\mathit{sn}} . 
 
 
 
 When the context is clear, for simplicity, we might abbreviate  Equation   1  using a set notation into  A i 𝑠𝑛 = A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 ⊎ { τ } A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}=A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1}\uplus\{\tau\} , where  ⊎ \uplus  denotes the disjoint set union. 
 
 
 Lemma B.5   (Transfer associated with a valid UA) . 
 
 Consider a valid UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 > 0 \mathit{sn}>0  issued by a process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty) 12 12 
                     12 
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                   The sequence number  𝑠𝑛 = 0 \mathit{sn}=0  is excluded as the genesis AP  σ i 0 \sigma_{i}^{0}  does not have a corresponding transfer. .
The existence of  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  implies that there exists a valid transfer  τ i 𝑠𝑛 \tau_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  issued by  p i p_{i}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  ( Definition   B.4 ). Furthermore this transfer is unique for a given  p i p_{i}  and  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn} . We say that  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is associated with  τ i 𝑠𝑛 \tau_{i}^{\mathit{sn}} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider a valid UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 > 0 \mathit{sn}>0  issued by a process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty).
By  Definition   B.1 , there exists a valid AP  σ i 𝑠𝑛 \sigma_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for  p i p_{i}  so that  𝐴𝑐𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡𝑈𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒 . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ i 𝑠𝑛 , ⟨ A i 𝑠𝑛 , ⋆ ⟩ , 𝑠𝑛 , i ) \mathit{AccountUpdate}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{i}^{\mathit{sn}},\langle A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}},\star\rangle,\mathit{sn},i) .
By AP-Knowledge-Soundness, and by definition of the  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  predicate for  𝐴𝑐𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡𝑈𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒 \mathit{AccountUpdate}  objects ( Algorithm   2 ), the prover  p i p_{i}  must know some  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 = ⟨ ⋆ , π i , 𝑜𝑙𝑑 ​ _ ​ 𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑒 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ⟩ \mathit{data}=\langle\star,\pi_{i},\mathit{old\_state\_data}\rangle  satisfying  P A ​ ( ⟨ A i 𝑠𝑛 , ⋆ ⟩ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 ) \mathit{P_{A}}(\langle A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}},\star\rangle,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}) .
Due to  algorithm   2  of the code of Predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  ( Algorithm   2 ), this implies that  𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑉𝑎𝑙𝑖𝑑𝑖𝑡𝑦 . 𝗓𝗄𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( π i , 𝑜𝑙𝑑 ​ _ ​ 𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑒 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ⊕ ⟨ A i 𝑠𝑛 , ⋆ , 𝑠𝑛 ⟩ ) \mathit{TransferValidity}.\textup{$\mathsf{zkp\_verify}$}(\pi_{i},\mathit{old\_state\_data}\oplus\langle A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}},\star,\mathit{sn}\rangle)  is true, where  𝑜𝑙𝑑 ​ _ ​ 𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑒 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 = ⟨ A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 , ⋆ ⟩ \mathit{old\_state\_data}=\langle A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1},\star\rangle .
As a result, by ZKP-Knowledge-Soundness and by construction of the  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  predicate of the ZKP object  𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑉𝑎𝑙𝑖𝑑𝑖𝑡𝑦 \mathit{TransferValidity}  ( Algorithm   2 ), the prover  p i p_{i}  must know some  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data}  containing a transfer  τ = ⟨ 𝑠𝑛𝑑 , v , 𝑟𝑐𝑣 , 𝑠𝑛 ′ ⟩ \tau=\langle\mathit{snd},v,\mathit{rcv},\mathit{sn}^{\prime}\rangle  variable (fifth parameter at  algorithm   2  of  Algorithm   2 ) such that  P 𝑍𝐾 ​ ( ⟨ A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 , ⋆ ⟩ ⊕ ⟨ A i 𝑠𝑛 , ⋆ , 𝑠𝑛 ⟩ , 𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \mathit{P_{ZK}}(\langle A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1},\star\rangle\oplus\langle A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}},\star,\mathit{sn}\rangle,\mathit{secret\_data})  is true (if  i = 𝑠𝑛𝑑 i=\mathit{snd} , then  𝑠𝑛 = 𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sn}=\mathit{sn}^{\prime}  due to the check at  algorithm   2  of  Algorithm   2 , otherwise if  i = 𝑟𝑐𝑣 i=\mathit{rcv} ,  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  and  𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sn}^{\prime}  can be different). Furthermore, due to  algorithm   2 , and by  Lemma   B.2 ,  A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1}  is the valid UA at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 − 1 \mathit{sn}-1  for process  p i p_{i} .
This observation, together with  algorithm   2  of  Algorithm   2  implies that  τ \tau  is a valid transfer issued by  p i p_{i}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn} . Because  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  and  A i 𝑠𝑛 − 1 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}-1}  are unique for  p i p_{i}  at their respective sequence number,  algorithm   2  further yields that  τ \tau  is the sole transfer that is valid for  p i p_{i}  and  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary B.6   (Sequence of preceding transfers of a valid UA) . 
 
 Consider a valid UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 ≥ 0 \mathit{sn}\geq 0  issued by a process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty).
 A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  can be associated with a unique sequence of preceding transfers, denoted  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠 ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ) = ( τ i 1 , . . , τ i 𝑠𝑛 ) \mathit{precedingTrans}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}})=(\tau_{i}^{1},..,\tau_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}) , so that each  τ i k \tau_{i}^{k}  is a valid transfer issued by  p i p_{i}  at sequence number  k k , with  1 ≤ k ≤ 𝑠𝑛 1\leq k\leq\mathit{sn} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The corollary follows from  Lemmas   B.3  and  B.5 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.7   (Prefix-ordering of of preceding transfers) . 
 
 The sequences of transfers produced for UAs issued by the same process  p i p_{i}  are prefix-ordered, i.e., if  A i 𝑠𝑛 1 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{1}}  and  A i 𝑠𝑛 2 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{2}}  are two valid UAs issued by  p i p_{i}  at sequence numbers  𝑠𝑛 1 \mathit{sn}_{1}  and  𝑠𝑛 2 \mathit{sn}_{2}  respectively such that  𝑠𝑛 1 ≤ 𝑠𝑛 2 \mathit{sn}_{1}\leq\mathit{sn}_{2} , then  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 1 ) \mathit{precedingTrans}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{1}})  is a prefix of  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 2 ) \mathit{precedingTrans}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}_{2}}) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from the unicity of a valid transfer for a given process and sequence number, as stated in  Lemma   B.5 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 For simplicity, in the following, we equate valid transfers with their corresponding  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  invocation.
More precisely, if  τ i 𝑠𝑛 \tau_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is a valid transfer at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for a process  p i p_{i} , we denote the  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  invocation corresponding to a  τ i 𝑠𝑛 \tau_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  by  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 𝑠𝑛𝑑 𝑠𝑛 ′ ​ ( v , 𝑟𝑐𝑣 ) \mathsf{transfer}_{\mathit{snd}}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}}(v,\mathit{rcv}) , where  𝑠𝑛𝑑 \mathit{snd}  and  𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sn}^{\prime}  are respectively the id of the sender and the sequence number of the transfer contained in  τ \tau .
In this case, we say that  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 𝑠𝑛𝑑 𝑠𝑛 ′ ​ ( v , 𝑟𝑐𝑣 ) \mathsf{transfer}_{\mathit{snd}}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}}(v,\mathit{rcv})  is the transfer invocation for prover  p i p_{i}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn} . 
 
 
 Definition B.8   (Sending UAs, receiving UAs, and null UAs) . 
 
 We distinguish two sorts of valid UAs that are produced in our system: sending UAs, and receiving UAs. 
 
 •  
 
 A valid  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is a sending UA if
the issuer  p i p_{i}  of  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is also the sender of the corresponding transfer  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( ⋆ , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(\star,j)  (where  i i  and  j j  can be different).
If the issuer  p i p_{i}  is correct, the sending UA has been produced at  algorithm   3  of  Algorithm   3 , during the  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ​ ( ) \textup{$\mathsf{process\_transfer}$}()  call at  algorithm   3 . 
 
 •  
 
 A valid  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is a receiving UA if
the issuer  p i p_{i}  of  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is also the receiver of the corresponding transfer  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j 𝑠𝑛 ′ ​ ( ⋆ , i ) \mathsf{transfer}_{j}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}}(\star,i)  (where  i i  and  j j  can be different, and  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  and  𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sn}^{\prime}  can be different).
If the issuer  p i p_{i}  is correct, the receiving UA is produced at  algorithm   3  of  Algorithm   3 , during the  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ​ ( ) \textup{$\mathsf{process\_transfer}$}()  call at  algorithm   3 . 
 
 
 Recall that a transfer invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , i ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,i)  from a process  p i p_{i}  to itself is allowed in our algorithm, which entails that the corresponding UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is both a sending UA and a receiving UA.
In this case,  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is said to be a null UA, and  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , i ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,i)  is said to be a null  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  invocation. 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.9   (Matching receiving UA to a sending UA) . 
 
 Any valid receiving UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  for some transfer  τ i = ⟨ j , ⋆ , i , 𝑠𝑛 τ i ⟩ \tau_{i}=\langle j,\star,i,\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}\rangle  from a receiver  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty) can be matched to a valid sending UA  A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  for the same transfer  τ i \tau_{i}  from the sender  p j ≠ p i p_{j}\neq p_{i}  (correct or faulty). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider a valid receiving UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  ( Definition   B.8 ) at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  for process  p i p_{i}  corresponding to some receiving transfer  τ i = ⟨ j , ⋆ , i , 𝑠𝑛 τ i ⟩ \tau_{i}=\langle j,\star,i,\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}\rangle  whose sender is  p j p_{j}  and receiver is  p i p_{i} . Two cases arise depending on whether  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is a null or non-null UA (cf.  Definition   B.8 ). 
 
 •  
 
 If  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is a null UA, it is both a sending UA and a receiving UA, and corresponds to a transfer  τ i = ⟨ i , ⋆ , i , 𝑠𝑛 ⟩ \tau_{i}=\langle i,\star,i,\mathit{sn}\rangle  from  p i p_{i}  to itself, thus fulfilling the lemma. 
 
 •  
 
 If  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is a non-null receiving UA, this  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is associated to a valid AP  σ i 𝑠𝑛 \sigma_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  ( Definition   B.1 ).  Algorithms   2  and  2  of  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  ( Algorithm   2 ) imply the existence of a valid UA  A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  for  p j p_{j}  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 τ i \mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}} , with  j ≠ i j\neq i  (since  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is non-null), and  τ i ∈ A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i \tau_{i}\in A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}} . 
 
 
 Consider the sequence  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i ) = ( A j k ) 0 ≤ k ≤ 𝑠𝑛 τ i \mathit{precedingUAs}(A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}})=(A_{j}^{k})_{0\leq k\leq\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  of UAs preceding  A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  ( Lemma   B.3 ), and the sequence  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠 ⁡ ( A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i ) = ( τ j k ) 0 < k ≤ 𝑠𝑛 τ i \mathit{precedingTrans}(A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}})=(\tau_{j}^{k})_{0<k\leq\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  of transfers preceding  A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  ( Corollary   B.6 ). By construction of the two sequences, each transfer  τ j k \tau_{j}^{k}  is valid for  p j p_{j}  at sequence number  k k , which implies 
 

 
 
 ∀ k ∈ [ 1 . . 𝑠𝑛 τ i ] : A j k = A j k − 1 ⊎ { τ j k } . \forall k\in[1..\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}]:A_{j}^{k}=A_{j}^{k-1}\uplus\{\tau_{j}^{k}\}. 
 
 (2) 
 
 Algorithm   2  of  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  ( Algorithm   2 ) implies that  A j 0 A_{j}^{0}  is empty. This observation and the above equation yield that  A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  contains exactly the transfers present in the sequence  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑇𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠 ⁡ ( A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i ) \mathit{precedingTrans}(A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}) . 
 
 
 Since  τ i ∈ A j 𝑠𝑛 τ i \tau_{i}\in A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}} , there exists some  k 0 ∈ [ 1 . . 𝑠𝑛 τ i ] k_{0}\in[1..\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}]  such that  τ i = τ j k 0 \tau_{i}=\tau_{j}^{k_{0}} . As the sender of  τ i \tau_{i}  is  p j p_{j} ,  algorithm   2  of  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  ( Algorithm   2 ) implies that  k 0 = 𝑠𝑛 τ i k_{0}=\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}} , and that  τ i = τ j 𝑠𝑛 τ i \tau_{i}=\tau_{j}^{\mathit{sn}_{\tau_{i}}}  by the unicity of a valid transfer for a given process and sequence number, as stated in  Lemma   B.5 .∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 Notations.   
For the remaining proofs, we introduce the following notations to manipulate the different notions we have discussed.
All notations are defined with respect to a particular global history  H H . 
 
 •  
 
 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ) = 𝖽𝖾𝖿 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 𝑠𝑛𝑑 𝑠𝑛 ′ ​ ( v , 𝑟𝑐𝑣 ) \mathit{transfer}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\mathsf{transfer}_{\mathit{snd}}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}}(v,\mathit{rcv})  is the transfer invocation corresponding to a valid UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  when  𝑠𝑛 > 0 \mathit{sn}>0  (see  Lemma   B.5 ).
 A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  might be a sending UA (in which case  i = 𝑠𝑛𝑑 i=\mathit{snd}  and  𝑠𝑛 = 𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sn}=\mathit{sn}^{\prime} ) or a receiving UA (in which case  i = 𝑟𝑐𝑣 i=\mathit{rcv} ). 
 
 •  
 
 𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , j ) ) = 𝖽𝖾𝖿 A i 𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sendingUA}(\mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is the sending UA issued by  p i p_{i}  corresponding to a valid transfer invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,j)  performed by  p i p_{i}  towards  p j p_{j} . By definition of a valid transfer and  Lemma   B.9 , this sending UA always exists, and by AP-Agreement, it is unique.

As a shortcut, we further say that  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn}  (the sequence number of the UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}} ) is the sequence number of the transfer invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,j) . 
 
 •  
 
 𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ) = 𝖽𝖾𝖿 A j 𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{sendingUA}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}}  is by extension the sending UA issued by  p j p_{j}  corresponding to a valid receiving UA  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  issued by  p i p_{i} .  𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ) \mathit{sendingUA}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}})  is a shortcut for  𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ) ) \mathit{sendingUA}(\mathit{transfer}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}})) .
In case  A i 𝑠𝑛 A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}  is a null UA ( Definition   B.8 ), we have  𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( A i 𝑠𝑛 ) = A i 𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sendingUA}(A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}})=A_{i}^{\mathit{sn}} . 
 
 •  
 
 𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , j ) ) = 𝖽𝖾𝖿 A j 𝑠𝑛 ′ \mathit{receivingUA}(\mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}A_{j}^{\mathit{sn}^{\prime}}  is, when it exists, the receiving UA issued by  p j p_{j}  corresponding to a valid transfer invocation  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,j)  performed by  p i p_{i}  towards  p j p_{j} . When both the sender  p i p_{i}  and the receiver  p j p_{j}  are correct, this receiving UA always exists because of the network’s reliability, and because the internal operation  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ( . . ) \textup{$\mathsf{process\_transfer}$}(..)  of  Algorithm   3  ( algorithms   3 – 3 ) does not contain any blocking operation. 
 
 
 When  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( v , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,j)  has no corresponding receiving UA (this can happen when either the sender  p i p_{i}  or the receiver  p j p_{j}  are Byzantine), by convention we define  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ( 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ( v , j ) ) = 𝖽𝖾𝖿 ⊥ \mathit{receivingUA}(\mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(v,j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\bot . 
 
 •  
 
 If a correct process  p i p_{i}  performs a  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ​ ( ) \mathsf{balance}_{i}()  invocation while its  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  variable has value  𝑠𝑛 \mathit{sn} , we denote it by  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( ) \mathsf{balance}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}() . 13 13 
                       13 
                      
                      
                      
                      
                      
                      
                      
                    Recall that we do not consider  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 ⁡ ( ) \mathsf{balance}()  invocations from Byzantine processes. 
 
 
 For simplicity, we denote by  𝗈𝗉 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( ) \mathsf{op}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}()  any invocation  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( ) \mathsf{balance}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}()  or  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}^{\mathit{sn}}(\star,\star) . 
 
 
 
 B.1.2  AT-Sequentiality of Algorithm  3 

 
 Let us consider any execution of  Algorithm   3 , captured as a global history  H = ( L 1 , … , L n ) H=(L_{1},...,L_{n}) .
To prove AT-Sequentiality ( Section   6.2.4 ), we must construct a mock history  H ^ \widehat{H}  of  H H  that preserves the local histories of correct processes and replaces the local histories  L j L_{j}  of Byzantine processes  p j p_{j}  by mock local histories  L ^ j \widehat{L}_{j}  so that  H ^ \widehat{H}  can be AT-sequenced. We construct  H ^ \widehat{H}  and the corresponding AT-sequence  S i S_{i}  incrementally by introducing several intermediary definitions and lemmas. 
 
 
 In the following,  C C  is the set of correct processes, and  B B  the set of Byzantine processes.
 C ∪ B = { p 1 , . . , p n } C\cup B=\{p_{1},..,p_{n}\} .
We construct the mock local histories  { L ^ j } p j ∈ B \{\widehat{L}_{j}\}_{p_{j}\in B}  of Byzantine processes as follows. 
 
 
 Definition B.10 . 
 
 We define the set  Σ \Sigma  of UAs defined recursively by the following rules. 
 
 •  
 
 Σ \Sigma  contains all the UAs issued by correct processes at  algorithm   3  of  Algorithm   3 . This includes both sending and receiving UAs. 
 
 •  
 
 If some receiving UA  A A  belongs to  Σ \Sigma , then  𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( A ) \mathit{sendingUA}(A)  also belongs to  Σ \Sigma , 
 

 
 
 { 𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( A ) ∣ A ​  is a valid receiving UA ∧ A ∈ Σ } ⊆ Σ . \big\{\mathit{sendingUA}(A)\mid A\text{ is a valid receiving UA}\wedge A\in\Sigma\big\}\subseteq\Sigma. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 If some UA  A A  belongs to  Σ \Sigma , then all UAs by the same issuer that precedes  A A  also belong to  A A , 
 

 
 
 ⋃ A ∈ Σ 𝗌𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( A ) ) ⊆ Σ . \bigcup_{A\in\Sigma}\mathsf{set}(\mathit{precedingUAs}(A))\subseteq\Sigma. 
 
 (3) 
 
 
 
 Σ \Sigma  can be seen as the causal transitive closure of the UAs issued by correct processes: it contains all the UAs that can be traced back to some UA issued by a correct process, either through a sending/receiving relationship as captured by  Lemma   B.9 , or through local precedence as captured by  Lemma   B.3 . 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.11   (Validity of UAs in  Σ \Sigma ) . 
 
 All UAs contained in  Σ \Sigma  are valid. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The remark holds because all UAs issued by correct processes are valid by construction, and results from the application of  Lemma   B.9  and  Lemma   B.3  when defining  𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathit{sendingUA}(\cdot)  and  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathit{precedingUAs}(\cdot)  respectively.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Definition B.12   (Mock history  H ^ \widehat{H} ) . 
 
 For a Byzantine process  p j ∈ B p_{j}\in B , we construct the set  Θ j \Theta_{j}  of transfer invocations whose sender is  p j p_{j} , and for which both a sending and a receiving UA can be found in  Σ \Sigma . More formally, we have 
 

 
 
 Θ j = 𝖽𝖾𝖿 { 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j 𝑠𝑛 ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) | ∃ A 1 , A 2 ∈ Σ : A 1 ​  is a sending UA ∧ A 2 ​  is a receiving UA ∧ 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A 1 ) = 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A 2 ) = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) } . \Theta_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\mathsf{transfer}_{j}^{\mathit{sn}}(\star,\star)\;\left|\;\begin{array}[]{c}\exists A_{1},A_{2}\in\Sigma:A_{1}\text{ is a sending UA}\;\wedge\;A_{2}\text{ is a receiving UA}\;\wedge\\
\;\mathit{transfer}(A_{1})=\mathit{transfer}(A_{2})=\mathsf{transfer}_{j}^{\mathit{sn}}(\star,\star)\end{array}\right.\right\}. 
 
 
 Let us note  𝑆𝑁 j \mathit{SN}_{j}  the set of the sequence numbers of the transfer invocations present in  Θ j \Theta_{j} .  𝑆𝑁 j \mathit{SN}_{j}  is a set of strictly positive integers (since  p j p_{j} ’s genesis UA  A j 0 A_{j}^{0}  created at  algorithm   1  of  Algorithm   1  does not have a corresponding transfer invocation),  𝑆𝑁 j ⊆ ℕ ∗ \mathit{SN}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{*} .
Note that, by AP-Agreement, each sequence number in  𝑆𝑁 j \mathit{SN}_{j}  corresponds to a single transfer invocation in  Θ j \Theta_{j} .
As a result, we can define the mock local history  L ^ j \widehat{L}_{j}  of  p j p_{j}  as the sequence of transfer invocations present in  Θ j \Theta_{j}  ordered by their sequence numbers, 
 

 
 
 L ^ j = 𝖽𝖾𝖿 ( 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j 𝑠𝑛 ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∈ Θ j ) 𝑠𝑛 ∈ 𝑆𝑁 j . \widehat{L}_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\big(\mathsf{transfer}_{j}^{\mathit{sn}}(\star,\star)\in\Theta_{j}\big)_{\mathit{sn}\in\mathit{SN}_{j}}. 
 
 
 Combining the mock local histories  ( L ^ j ) j ∈ B (\widehat{L}_{j})_{j\in B}  of Byzantine processes with the local histories  ( L i ) i ∈ C (L_{i})_{i\in C}  of correct processes yields a mock history  H ^ \widehat{H} . 
 
 
 
 Definition B.13   (Set  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i}  of operation perceived by a correct process  p i p_{i} ) . 
 
 Consider a correct process  p i p_{i} , and the set  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i}  containing all invocations made by  p i p_{i}  (to the operations  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  and  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance} ), and all transfer invocations by other processes (correct of faulty) present in  H ^ \widehat{H} . 
 

 
 
 𝒮 i = 𝗌𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( L i ) ∪ ⋃ j ∈ C ∖ { i } 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( L j ) ∪ ⋃ j ∈ B 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( L ^ j ) , \mathcal{S}_{i}=\mathsf{set}(L_{i})\cup\bigcup_{j\in C\setminus\{i\}}\mathsf{transferSet}(L_{j})\cup\bigcup_{j\in B}\mathsf{transferSet}(\widehat{L}_{j}), 
 
 (4) 
 
 where  C C  and  B B  are the sets of correct and Byzantine processes as defined above, respectively, and  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋𝖲𝖾𝗍 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathsf{transferSet}(\cdot)  is the notation introduced in  Section   4  to denote the set of transfer invocations contained in a sequence. 
 
 
 
 To produce a sequence  S i S_{i}  from the elements of  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i} , we construct a partial order  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  on  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i} . We do so incrementally, by introducing two binary relations,  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} . 
 
 •  
 
 ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  totally orders the invocation of  p i p_{i}  and ensures that incoming transfers received by a process  p j p_{j}  are ordered before the subsequent balance invocations (if  j = i j=i ) and outgoing transfers issued by  p j p_{j} . 
 
 •  
 
 ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  focuses specifically on  p i p_{i}  to guarantee that incoming transfers received by  p i p_{i}  are totally ordered with respect to  p i p_{i} ’s local invocation to  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathsf{balance}(\cdot) . 
 
 
 Distinguishing between  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  as intermediary steps towards  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  will in turn make it easier to prove that the  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  is acyclic (a necessary condition to show that it is a partial order). 
 
 
 Definition B.14   (Binary relations  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} ,  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} , and  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  on  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i} ) . 
 
 We define  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  on  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i}  as follows. 
 
 •  
 
 Inclusion of  p i \bm{p_{i}} ’s local order.   ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  respects the local process order  → i \rightarrow_{i}  of operations invoked by  p i p_{i} , i.e.,  → i ⊆ ↝ 1 i \rightarrow_{i}\subseteq\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} . 
 
 •  
 
 Ordering of incoming transfers. 
 
 –  
 
 For any process  p j p_{j} ,  t ⊳ ​ j = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , j ) ∈ 𝒮 i t_{\vartriangleright j}=\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}^{\star}(\star,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  a transfer invocation whose receiver is  p j p_{j} , and  t j ​ ⊳ = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∈ 𝒮 i t_{j\vartriangleright}=\mathsf{transfer}_{j}^{\star}(\star,\star)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  a transfer invocation by  p j p_{j}  such that  t ⊳ ​ j ≠ t j ​ ⊳ t_{\vartriangleright j}\neq t_{j\vartriangleright} , if  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( t ⊳ ​ j ) ∈ 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( 𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( t j ​ ⊳ ) ) \mathit{receivingUA}(t_{\vartriangleright j})\in\mathit{precedingUAs}(\mathit{sendingUA}(t_{j\vartriangleright})) , then  t ⊳ ​ j ​ ↝ 1 i ​ t j ​ ⊳ t_{\vartriangleright j}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}t_{j\vartriangleright} . 
 
 –  
 
 For transfer invocation  t ⊳ ​ i = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , i ) ∈ 𝒮 i t_{\vartriangleright i}=\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}^{\star}(\star,i)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  whose receiver is  p i p_{i} , and any invocation  b i = 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ​ ( ) / v b_{i}=\mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v  performed by  p i p_{i} , if the execution of  algorithm   3  that generated  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( t ⊳ ​ i ) \mathit{receivingUA}(t_{\vartriangleright i})  occurred before that of  algorithm   3  corresponding to  b i b_{i}  , then  t ⊳ ​ i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ b i t_{\vartriangleright i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}b_{i} . 
 
 
 
 
 We define  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  on  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i}  as follows. 
 
 •  
 
 Ordering of  p i \bm{p_{i}} ’s balance.  For any invocation  b i = 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ​ ( ) / v b_{i}=\mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v  performed by  p i p_{i} , and any transfer invocation  t ⊳ ​ i = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , i ) ∈ 𝒮 i t_{\vartriangleright i}=\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}^{\star}(\star,i)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  whose receiver is  p i p_{i} , if the execution of  algorithm   3  corresponding to  b i b_{i}  occurred before that of  algorithm   3  that generated  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( t ⊳ ​ i ) \mathit{receivingUA}(t_{\vartriangleright i}) , then  b i ​ ↝ 2 i ​ t ⊳ ​ i b_{i}\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}t_{\vartriangleright i} . 
 
 
 ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  is defined as the transitive closure of the union of  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} , i.e.,  ↝ i = ( ↝ 1 i ∪ ↝ 2 i ) + \rightsquigarrow_{i}=\big(\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\cup\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\big)^{+} . 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.15 . 
 
 ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  is acyclic. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The distributed system is implicitly embedded in some physical time, which we assume is Newtonian and linear. 14 14 
                     14 
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                    
                  Note that the proof also holds in the context of special relativity, even though there may not be an absolute global clock ordering the events of the system.
This is because any observer in the system still sees the events in a linear (total) order that may, however, differ from one observer to the next.
For this proof, it suffices to take any observer ( e.g. ,  p i p_{i} ) as a frame of reference to construct the timestamps.
But because of the causality principle, two causally-linked events are necessarily seen in the same order, no matter the chosen frame of reference.
 
We associate each element  x x  of  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i}  with a timestamp  τ ⁡ ( x ) \tau(x)  from this physical time as follows: 
 
 •  
 
 if  x = 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ​ ( ) / v x=\mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v  is a balance invocation by  p i p_{i} ,  τ ⁡ ( x ) \tau(x)  is the time point at which  algorithm   3  of  Algorithm   3  is executed; 
 
 •  
 
 if  x = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) x=\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}^{\star}(\star,\star)  is a transfer invocation,  τ ⁡ ( x ) \tau(x)  is the time point at which  𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( x ) \mathit{sendingUA}(x)  was created. 
 
 
 Consider  x , y ∈ 𝒮 i x,y\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  such that  x ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y x\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y .
At least one of the following cases holds. 
 
 •  
 
 Case 1 (Inclusion of  p i p_{i} ’s local order):   x x  and  y y  are both invoked by  p i p_{i}  with  x → i y x\rightarrow_{i}y . Because  p i p_{i}  is sequential, by definition of  → i \rightarrow_{i}  we have  τ ⁡ ( x ) < τ ⁡ ( y ) \tau(x)<\tau(y) . 
 
 •  
 
 Case 2 (Ordering of incoming transfers): 
 
 –  
 
 x = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , j ) ∈ 𝒮 i x=\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}^{\star}(\star,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  is a transfer invocation whose receiver is some process  p j p_{j} , and  y = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∈ 𝒮 i y=\mathsf{transfer}_{j}^{\star}(\star,\star)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  a transfer invocation by  p j p_{j} , such that  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( x ) ∈ 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( 𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( y ) ) \mathit{receivingUA}(x)\in\mathit{precedingUAs}(\mathit{sendingUA}(y)) . This last inclusion means that  𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( y ) \mathit{sendingUA}(y)  can be linked recursively to  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( x ) \mathit{receivingUA}(x)  using proofs issues by  p j p_{j} , the predicates  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  and  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}} , and AP-Knowledge-Soundness and ZKP-Knowledge-Soundness. AP-Knowledge-Soundness and ZKP-Knowledge-Soundness both imply that  p j p_{j}  must know the secret data required to issue a proof  before  the proof is generated. As a result  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( x ) ∈ 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( 𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( y ) ) \mathit{receivingUA}(x)\in\mathit{precedingUAs}(\mathit{sendingUA}(y))  implies that  τ ⁡ ( x ) < τ ⁡ ( y ) \tau(x)<\tau(y) . 
 
 –  
 
 x = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ​ ( ⋆ , i ) ∈ 𝒮 i x=\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}^{\star}(\star,i)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  is a transfer invocation whose receiver is  p i p_{i} ,  y = 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ​ ( ) / v y=\mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v  is a  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  invocation performed by  p i p_{i} , such that the execution of  algorithm   3  that generated  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( x ) \mathit{receivingUA}(x)  occurred before that of  algorithm   3  corresponding to  y y . The same reasoning as above yields that  τ ⁡ ( x ) < τ ⁡ ( y ) \tau(x)<\tau(y) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 We conclude that  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  respects the timestamps assigned by  τ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \tau(\cdot) . Assuming physical time is acyclic, this implies that  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  is also acyclic.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.16 . 
 
 ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  is acyclic. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  is a simple bipartite binary relation between  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  invocations on one side, and  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  invocations on the other, hence it cannot contain cycles, and is therefore acyclic.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.17 . 
 
 ↝ 1 i ∪ ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\cup\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  is acyclic. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is by contradiction. Assume  ↝ 1 i ∪ ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\cup\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  contains at least one cycle. Consider the shortest such cycle  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}} . Because  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  are both acyclic ( Lemmas   B.15  and  B.16 ),  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}}  must involve both  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} . 
 
 
 Consider one of the  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  ordering in  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}} . By definition of  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} , this ordering is of the form  b i ​ ↝ 2 i ​ t ⊳ ​ i b_{i}\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}t_{\vartriangleright i} , where  b i b_{i}  is a balance invocation by  p i p_{i}  and any  t ⊳ ​ i t_{\vartriangleright i}  is a transfer invocation whose receiver is  p i p_{i} . Consider the successor  y y  of  t ⊳ ​ i t_{\vartriangleright i}  in  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}} . By construction of  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} ,  t ⊳ ​ i ​ ↝ 2 i ​ y t_{\vartriangleright i}\not\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y , which implies  t ⊳ ​ i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y t_{\vartriangleright i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y . 
 
 
 By definition of  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} ,  t ⊳ ​ i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y t_{\vartriangleright i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y  leads to two cases. 
 
 •  
 
 Case 1:   t ⊳ ​ i → i y t_{\vartriangleright i}\rightarrow_{i}y . In this case,  y ∈ L i y\in L_{i}  by definition of  → i \rightarrow_{i} ,  p i p_{i} ’s local process order. 
 
 •  
 
 Case 2:   t ⊳ ​ i ↛ i y t_{\vartriangleright i}\not\rightarrow_{i}y . In this case,  t ⊳ ​ i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y t_{\vartriangleright i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y  resulted from the rule  Ordering of incoming transfers .  y y  is therefore either a  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  or  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  operation invoked by  p i p_{i} ,  i.e. , we also have  y ∈ L i y\in L_{i} . 
 
 
 
 
 We therefore have  b i ​ ↝ 2 i ​ t ⊳ ​ i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y b_{i}\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}t_{\vartriangleright i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y , with  b i , y ∈ L i b_{i},y\in L_{i} .
The definitions of  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  imply that the invocation of  b i b_{i}  by  p i p_{i}  precedes in time the generation of  𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴 ⁡ ( t ⊳ ​ i ) \mathit{receivingUA}(t_{\vartriangleright i})  also by  p i p_{i} , which itself precedes the invocation of  y y  still by  p i p_{i}  (either directly in the case of  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i} , and if  y y  is a  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  invocation, or using the same argument on cryptographic proofs as in  Lemma   B.15  if  y y  is a  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer} ).
By definition of the process order  → i \rightarrow_{i} , this leads to  b i → i y b_{i}\rightarrow_{i}y , and hence  b i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y b_{i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y .
We can, therefore, remove  t ⊳ ​ i t_{\vartriangleright i}  from  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}} , and replace  b i ​ ↝ 2 i ​ t ⊳ ​ i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y b_{i}\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}t_{\vartriangleright i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y  by  b i ​ ↝ 1 i ​ y b_{i}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}y , thus creating a cycle  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 ′ \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}}^{\prime}  in  ↝ 1 i ∪ ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\cup\overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  with one less element than  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}} .
This is a contradiction as  𝒞 𝗆𝗂𝗇 \mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{min}}  was assumed to be the shortest.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary B.18 . 
 
 ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  is a partial order on  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  is transitive by construction. By  Lemma   B.17 , it is the transitive closure of an acyclic relation and is, therefore, also acyclic.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.19 . 
 
 Each element of the poset ( 𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i} , ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i} ) only has a finite number of predecessors. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from the sequential nature of processes (and in particular that all processes have a starting point in their execution), the link between  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and  ↝ 2 i \overset{\textbf{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  and physical time ( Lemmas   B.15  and  B.16 ), and the (implicit) assumption that processes only take a finite number of local steps per unit of time.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Definition B.20   (Sequence  S i S_{i} ) . 
 
 By  Lemma   B.19  the poset ( 𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i} , ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i} ) can be sorted topologically into a sequence. We define  S i S_{i}  as one of the topological sorts of ( 𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i} , ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i} ). We note  → S i \rightarrow_{S_{i}}  the total order induced by  S i S_{i}  on its elements. 
 
 
 
 In the following, we will use the function  𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \mathit{total}(\cdot,\cdot)  introduced in  Section   4  to compute the account balance of a process resulting from the transfer it sends and receives. Given a process  p j p_{j}  and a set of operations invocations  O O ,  𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( j , O ) \mathit{total}(j,O)  is defined as 
 

 
 
 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( j , O ) = 𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 j + ( Σ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ​ ( v , j ) ∈ O ​ v ) − ( Σ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j ​ ( v , ⋆ ) ∈ O ​ v ) . \mathit{total}(j,O)=\mathtt{init}_{j}+\big(\Sigma_{\mathsf{transfer}_{\star}(v,j)\in O}\,v\big)-\big(\Sigma_{\mathsf{transfer}_{j}(v,\star)\in O}\,v\big). 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.21 . 
 
 ∀ b i = 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ( ) / v ∈ S i : v = 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ( i , { o ∈ S i ∣ o → S i b i } ) . \Forall b_{i}=\mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v\in S_{i}:v=\mathit{total}(i,\{o\in S_{i}\mid o\rightarrow_{S_{i}}b_{i}\}).

 Consider  b i = 𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 i ​ ( ) / v ∈ S i b_{i}=\mathsf{balance}_{i}()/v\in S_{i}  a balance operation performed by a correct process  p i p_{i}  that returns a value  v v .
Define the sets  I i I_{i}  and  R i R_{i}  as follows: 
 
 •

By construction of  Algorithm   3 ,  p i p_{i}  generated a sending UA at  algorithm   3  for each of these transfers. 
 
 •  
 
 R i R_{i}  is the set of  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer} s  received  by  p i p_{i}  whose receiving UA was generated by  p i p_{i}  (at  algorithm   3 ) before invoking  b i b_{i} . 
 
 
 
 
 Because  p i p_{i}  is correct, it executes  Algorithm   3 , which ensures by construction (in particular due to  algorithms   3 ,  3 ,  3  and  3 ) that 
 

 
 
 v = 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( i , I i ∪ R i ) , v=\mathit{total}(i,I_{i}\cup R_{i}), 
 
 (5) 
 
 taking into account that the effects of null transfers on  i i  cancel out in the definition of  𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 \mathit{total} . 
 
 
 Consider now  T i ​ ⊳ T_{i\vartriangleright}  the subset of transfers present in  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i}  that  p i p_{i}  issued, and  T ⊳ ​ i T_{\vartriangleright i}  the subset of transfers present in  𝒮 i \mathcal{S}_{i}  that  p i p_{i}  received,  i.e. ,

 These last two equalities together with  Equations   8  and  5  yield the lemma. 15 15

 Lemma B.22 . 
 
 ∀ j ∈ [ 0 . . n ] , ∀ t j ​ ⊳ = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j ( v , ⋆ ) ∈ S i : v ≤ 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ( j , { o ∈ S i ∣ o → S i t j ​ ⊳ } ) . \Forall j\in[0..n],\Forall t_{j\vartriangleright}=\mathsf{transfer}_{j}(v,\star)\in S_{i}:v\leq\mathit{total}\big(j,\{o\in S_{i}\mid o\rightarrow_{S_{i}}t_{j\vartriangleright}\}\big).

 I t j ​ ⊳ S i = { 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j ⋆ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) ∈ S i ∣ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j ⋆ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) → S i t j ​ ⊳ ∨ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j ⋆ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) = t j ​ ⊳ } . I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}=\big\{\mathsf{transfer}^{\star}_{j}(\star,\star)\in S_{i}\mid\mathsf{transfer}^{\star}_{j}(\star,\star)\rightarrow_{S_{i}}t_{j\vartriangleright}\vee\mathsf{transfer}^{\star}_{j}(\star,\star)=t_{j\vartriangleright}\big\}. 
 
 
 
 •

 R t j ​ ⊳ S i = { 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ( j , ⋆ ) ∈ S i ∣ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ⋆ ⋆ ( j , ⋆ ) → S i t j ​ ⊳ } . {R_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}}=\big\{\mathsf{transfer}^{\star}_{\star}(j,\star)\in S_{i}\mid\mathsf{transfer}^{\star}_{\star}(j,\star)\rightarrow_{S_{i}}t_{j\vartriangleright}\big\}.

 Remark B.22.1 . 
 
 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ( j , I t j ​ ⊳ S i ∪ R t j ​ ⊳ S i ) = 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ( j , { o ∈ S i ∣ o → S i t j ​ ⊳ } ) − v . \mathit{total}(j,I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}\cup{R_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}})=\mathit{total}(j,\{o\in S_{i}\mid o\rightarrow_{S_{i}}t_{j\vartriangleright}\})-v. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This directly follows from the definition of  𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \mathit{total}(\cdot,\cdot) ,  I t j ​ ⊳ S i I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}  and  R t j ​ ⊳ S i {R_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}} .

 Consider  𝑆𝑁 t j ​ ⊳ S i \mathit{SN}_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}  the set of the sequence numbers of the transfers present in  I t j ​ ⊳ S i I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}} ,  i.e. ,

However, because  ↝ i \rightsquigarrow_{i}  does not impose strong constraints on the order of the transfer invocations issued by a process other than  p i p_{i} ,  I t j ​ ⊳ S i I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}  might also contain transfer invocations that have a higher sequence number than  t j ​ ⊳ t_{j\vartriangleright} .

 Define  𝑠𝑛 j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 \mathit{sn}^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}  as the highest sequence number contained in  𝑆𝑁 t j ​ ⊳ S i \mathit{SN}_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}} ,

\mathsf{transfer}^{\star}_{\star}(j,\star)\neq\mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})\end{array}\right.\right\}. 
 
 (14) 
 
 
 
 
 
 Let  v 𝗆𝖺𝗑 v_{\mathsf{max}}  denote the amount of the transfer invocation  𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) \mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}) ,  i.e. ,  𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) = 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j 𝑠𝑛 j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ​ ( v 𝗆𝖺𝗑 , ⋆ ) \mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})=\mathsf{transfer}_{j}^{\mathit{sn}^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}}(v_{\mathsf{max}},\star) .
(The notation  𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) \mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})  was defined at the end of  Section   B.1.1 ) 
 
 
 Remark B.22.2 . 
 
 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( j , I t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ∪ R t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) = 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( j , ( I t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ∖ { 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) } ) ∪ R t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) − v 𝗆𝖺𝗑 . \mathit{total}\left(j,I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}\cup{R_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}}\right)=\mathit{total}\left(j,\left(I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}\setminus\left\{\mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})\right\}\right)\cup{R_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}}\right)-v_{\mathsf{max}}.

 Similarly to  Remark   B.22.1 , the remark follows from the fact that  𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) ∈ I t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 \mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})\in I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}  and  𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) ∉ R t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 \mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})\not\in{R_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}} , the definition of  v 𝗆𝖺𝗑 v_{\mathsf{max}} , and that of  𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \mathit{total}(\cdot,\cdot) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark B.22.3 . 
 
 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 ⁡ ( j , ( I t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ∖ { 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) } ) ∪ R t j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) ≥ v 𝗆𝖺𝗑 . \mathit{total}\left(j,\left(I_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}\setminus\left\{\mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})\right\}\right)\cup{R_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{\mathsf{max}}}\right)\geq v_{\mathsf{max}}.

 This follows from a recursion on the sequence of (valid) UAs  𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑒𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔𝑈𝐴𝑠 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) \mathit{precedingUAs}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})  that precede  A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}  ( Lemma   B.3 ), as well as the checks and updates contained in the  P 𝑍𝐾 \mathit{P_{ZK}}  predicate ( Algorithm   2 ). This includes the check at  algorithm   2  that a sending process must have enough funds, and the updates performed on the sender’s balance at  algorithm   2  (for null UAs),  algorithm   2  (for non-null sending UAs), and  algorithm   2  (for non-null receiving UAs).
∎

 Remark B.22.5 .

∎

 (15) 
 
 
 
 Furthermore, because  𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) ∈ 𝒮 i \mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})\in\mathcal{S}_{i} , we have  t ⊳ ​ j ′ ∈ 𝒮 i t_{\vartriangleright j}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  (either because the sender of  t ⊳ ​ j t_{\vartriangleright j}  is correct, or by construction of  Σ \Sigma  if it is faulty, see  Equations   4  and  3 ).
Remember that  A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}  is associated with sequence number  𝑠𝑛 j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ∈ 𝑆𝑁 t j ​ ⊳ S i \mathit{sn}^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}\in\mathit{SN}_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}  ( Equation   11 ). Because  𝑆𝑁 t j ​ ⊳ S i \mathit{SN}_{t_{j\vartriangleright}}^{S_{i}}  only contains the sequence numbers of transfers whose sender is  p j p_{j} ,  𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ⁡ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) \mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright})  must be some transfer  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 j 𝑠𝑛 j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ​ ( ⋆ , ⋆ ) \mathsf{transfer}^{\mathit{sn}^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}}_{j}(\star,\star)  sent from  p j p_{j} .
As a result,  t ⊳ ​ j ′ ∈ 𝒮 i t_{\vartriangleright j}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}  together with  Equation   15  mean that the condition for the rule  Ordering of incoming transfers  in the definition of  ↝ 1 i \overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}  is met, yielding  t ⊳ ​ j ′ ​ ↝ 1 i ​ 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ​ ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) t_{\vartriangleright j}^{\prime}\overset{\textbf{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}_{i}\mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}) , and therefore  t ⊳ ​ j ′ ↝ i 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) t_{\vartriangleright j}^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow_{i}\mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}) , and 
 

 
 
 t ⊳ ​ j ′ → S i 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟 ( A j ​ ⊳ 𝗆𝖺𝗑 ) . t_{\vartriangleright j}^{\prime}\rightarrow_{S_{i}}\mathit{transfer}(A^{\mathsf{max}}_{j\vartriangleright}).

 B.1.3  AT-Termination of Algorithm  3 

 
 See  6.2.4 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The  𝖻𝖺𝗅𝖺𝗇𝖼𝖾 \mathsf{balance}  operation of  Algorithm   3  terminates trivially.
Furthermore, the only blocking instruction of the  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  operation of  Algorithm   3  is the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  operation at  algorithm   3 , in the  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{process\_transfer}  internal operation (called at  algorithm   3 ).
This  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  operation terminates by AP-Termination, so the  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 \mathsf{transfer}  operation also terminates.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 B.2  Proof of quasi-anonymity 

 
 See  6.2.4 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 To determine if the adversary  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  can obtain any information on the receiver  p j p_{j}  of any  𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 i ​ ( ⋆ , j ) \mathsf{transfer}_{i}(\star,j)  invocation given a global history  H H , we analyze the content of all message types produced by  Algorithm   3 .
Our algorithm features 2 message types with the following content: 
 
 1.  
 
 Accumulator update sent by a (sender or receiver) process  p i p_{i}  publicly to the network in the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  operation at  algorithm   3 : value  v = ⟨ A i ′ , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ′ ⟩ v=\langle A_{i}^{\prime},\mathit{bal\_c}_{i}^{\prime}\rangle  and data  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 = ⟨ σ i , π , 𝑠𝑛 i + 1 , ⟨ A i , A i ′ , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ′ ⟩ ⟩ \mathit{data}=\langle\sigma_{i},\pi,\mathit{sn}_{i}+1,\langle A_{i},A^{\prime}_{i},\mathit{bal\_c}_{i},\mathit{bal\_c}^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\rangle ; 
 
 2.  
 
 Message from a sender  p 𝑠𝑛𝑑 p_{\mathit{snd}}  to a receiver  p 𝑟𝑐𝑣 p_{\mathit{rcv}} :  ⟨ τ , σ 𝑠𝑛𝑑 , A 𝑠𝑛𝑑 , w 𝑠𝑛𝑑 , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c 𝑠𝑛𝑑 ⟩ \langle\tau,\sigma_{\mathit{snd}},A_{\mathit{snd}},w_{\mathit{snd}},\mathit{bal\_c}_{\mathit{snd}}\rangle . 
 
 
 
 
 Note that sender-receiver messages (item 2) are exchanged using the  𝗋𝖺 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{ra\_send}  operation, which ensures receiver anonymity and confidentiality (see Section  3 ).
Therefore, only accumulator updates (item 1) need to be considered.
C-Hiding guarantees that the commitments  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i \mathit{bal\_c}_{i}  and  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ′ \mathit{bal\_c}^{\prime}_{i}  do not leak any data on the corresponding committed values.
Similarly, UA-Indistinguishability guarantees that the accumulators  A i A_{i}  and  A i ′ A^{\prime}_{i}  do not leak any data on their accumulated sets, and ZKP-Zero-Knowledge ensures that  π \pi  does not leak any data on its secret input  𝑠𝑒𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡 ​ _ ​ 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{secret\_data} .
Finally, the sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i + 1 \mathit{sn}_{i}+1  does not reveal whether  p i p_{i}  is a sender or receiver, only the number of transfers (debits or credits) in  A i A_{i} .
Thus,  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  does not learn any information from accumulator updates. 
 
 
 We conclude that without any information on the content of transfers already included or newly added to some history  H H ,  𝐴𝑑𝑣 \mathit{Adv}  cannot deduce the recipient of a transfer more accurately than random,  i.e. , with a probability of less than  min ⁡ ( ( 1 / n ) + ϵ ⁡ ( λ ) , 1 ) \min((1/n)+\epsilon(\lambda),1) , where  ϵ \epsilon  is the statistical negligible function and  λ \lambda  is the security parameter of the system.
∎ 
 
 
 
 See  6.2.4 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof follows the same reasoning as that of  Section   6.2.4 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix C  Implementations of the Schemes of Section  5  / Appendix  A 

 
 C.1  Preliminaries 

 
 C.1.1  Groups of unknown order 

 
 Our choice of UA  [ 7 ]  implementation requires the use of groups of unknown order (as defined in  [ 15 ] ) in which the Strong RSA assumption, the Low Order assumption and the Adaptive Root assumption holds.
Concrete examples of groups of unknown order are RSA groups, ideal class groups and hyperelliptic Jacobians. However, only class groups and hyperelliptic Jacobians are efficient in the trustless setup model.
In particular,  [ 18 ]  provides algorithms to trustlessly setup genus- 3 3  hyperelliptic Jacobians of unknown order with  3392 3392 -bits field elements for  128 128  bits of security, which is close to the  3072 3072 -bits elements of RSA groups generated using a trusted setup for the same bit-security. They also provide a new compression algorithm for class groups, reaching  128 128 -bits of security with  5088 5088 -bits elements. 
 
 
 
 
 C.2  Accumulator 

 
 For our accumulator (specified in  Section   A.2 ), we opt for the trustless extension of universal RSA accumulators in groups of unknown order from  [ 7 ] . While RSA accumulators typically require the accumulated elements to be prime numbers, one can use a hash function with prime domain to accumulate arbitrary elements.
The scheme features constant size public parameters, accumulator digest and proofs of (non-)membership. Moreover, (non-)membership proofs can be verified in constant time. The scheme can thus be described as succinct.
Note that when adding a new element  e e  to an accumulator  A A , the membership proof of  e e  is the value of  A A  before  e e  was added. Therefore, as a process only needs the membership proof of a transfer when updating the accumulator and creating the ZKP of its account update, membership proof generation is done in constant time in  Algorithm   3 . 
 
 
 C.2.1  Hashing to primes 

 
 An RSA accumulator can only contain prime numbers.
To overcome this limitation, one must use a hash-to-prime function  H 𝖯𝗋𝗂𝗆𝖾𝗌 : { 0 , 1 } ∗ → 𝖯𝗋𝗂𝗆𝖾𝗌 ⁡ ( λ ) H_{\mathsf{Primes}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathsf{Primes}(\lambda) , where  𝖯𝗋𝗂𝗆𝖾𝗌 ⁡ ( λ ) \mathsf{Primes}(\lambda)  is the set of primes smaller than  2 λ 2^{\lambda} .  H 𝖯𝗋𝗂𝗆𝖾𝗌 H_{\mathsf{Primes}}  must be deterministic and collision-resistant to prevent an element from being mapped to multiple primes or multiple elements from being hashed into the same prime.
A common method of hashing to prime numbers is to combine a pseudo-random function ( e.g. , hash function) with a probabilistic primality test. The resulting function consists of successively hashing the concatenation of the data to hash with an incremented counter until finding the smallest nonce that yields a prime number. 
 
 
 
 C.2.2  Experimentations 

 
 All our tests are executed sequentially on a core i9-11950H processor. Our implementation is coded in C++ using the GNU MP Bignum library.
We instantiated  H 𝖯𝗋𝗂𝗆𝖾𝗌 H_{\mathsf{Primes}}  using SHA3 and a probabilistic Miller-Rabin primality test with  80 80  rounds, resulting in an error probability of  4 − 80 4^{-80} .
The output of SHA3 is truncated to  264 264  bits, which is sufficient to obtain  128 128  bits of security as the prime counting function estimates that there are at least  2 256 2^{256}  primes in  [ 0 , 2 264 ] [0,2^{264}] . We measure an average time of  9 9  ms to hash a single element to a  264 264  bits prime.
We also implement a  2048 2048 -bit RSA accumulator.  Table   4  shows the performance of proof generation and verification. We observe that while (non-)membership proof generation from "scratch" scales linearly with the size of the accumulated set, membership proof generation during element addition is computed in constant time as element accumulation consists of a single exponentiation of the RSA digest by the newly added element. Similarly, we observe that (non-)membership proof verification is constant time, as it also consists of a single group exponentiation.
