 Table 4:  Performance of accumulator proof generation and verification for various set sizes  | S | |S| . 
 
 
 
 
 C.3  Commitment scheme 

 
 In our system, each process creates a hiding commitment of a single value (the balance of its account). This commitment can be based on any constant-size commitment scheme with trustless setup. For example, one could use Pedersen commitments in groups of unknown order (or elliptic curves  [ 21 ] ), which are unconditionally hiding and computationally binding. Conversely, other schemes such as ElGamal commitments  [ 24 ]  could be used to obtain commitments that are unconditionally binding but computationally hiding. Note that it is impossible for a commitment scheme to be both unconditionally binding and unconditionally hiding. 
 
 
 
 C.4  Transparent zk-SNARK with time-optimal prover 

 
 For our zk-SNARK scheme, we choose Spartan  [ 42 ] , which is both transparent (trustless setup) and prover time-optimal. 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix D  A consensus-free quorum-based Agreement Proof implementation 

 
 In this section, we provide an implementation of the agreement proof scheme of  Section   6.1  that does not rely on consensus, uses succinct cryptography, and has a storage of  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits per process and an overall communication of  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n)  bits.
Our AP implementation leverages threshold digital signatures (see  Section   D.1 ) to create constant-size ( i.e. ,  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda) -bit) agreement proofs  σ \sigma  ( i.e. , quorums of signatures) and to verify them without the set of all public keys. 
 
 
 D.1  Threshold signatures 

 
 Threshold signatures are a family of aggregated digital signatures that are produced in a system of  n n  processes, by having at least  k k  out of  n n  processes (the threshold) produce individual signatures for the same message, which we call  intermediary signatures .
Once these intermediary signatures have been produced, they are aggregated (typically by a coordinating process) into a fixed-size aggregated signature, called the threshold signature  σ \sigma .
We call a threshold signature with a threshold of  k k  signers out of  n n  processes a  k k -threshold signature .
Unlike multi-signature schemes, which keep track of the identities of their signers, classical threshold signature schemes use a protocol called distributed key generation (DKG) to distribute the secret key of a signature scheme across processes. Processes then interact to reconstruct a single signature that can be verified against a single system-wide public key.
The computation of the individual shares of the secret key and the derivation of the system-wide public key are typically performed during the setup phase of the system.
Naturally, a  k k -threshold signature for a message  m m  is valid if and only if it aggregates  k k  valid distinct intermediary signatures for  m m . 
 
 
 Various threshold signature schemes have been built on top of asymmetric schemes such as RSA, Schnorr, BLS or ECDSA  [ 8 ,  43 ,  31 ,  40 ,  16 ,  44 ] .
Furthermore, the verification algorithm of most threshold versions of a classical signature scheme remains unchanged and can thus be efficiently verified independently of the number of co-signers using the system-wide public key.
For example, the asynchronous DKG protocol of  [ 16 ]  can be used with DLOG-based threshold cryptosystems such as BLS  [ 8 ]  or Schnorr  [ 40 ,  5 ]  to provide  k k -threshold signatures with high-threshold values ( k ∈ [ t , n − t − 1 ] k\in[t,n-t-1] ). 
 
 
 D.1.1  Succinctness of threshold signatures 

 
 Efficient high-threshold signature implementations ( e.g. ,  [ 8 ,  40 ,  5 ] ) guarantee that, for a fixed  λ \lambda  and any number of signers  k k , the size and verification time of a threshold signature is equivalent to that of a single intermediary signature.
Unlike classical signature schemes and multi-signature schemes, which require knowing the public keys of all the signers of a quorum of signatures, threshold signature schemes only require knowing one global public key for the system for verifying a quorum of signatures.
In other words, given the maximum number of signers  n n  in the system, instead of having quorum signatures of size  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n)  bits with classical signature schemes or of size  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits with multi-signature schemes (as the set of signers of a quorum can be encoded in a bit vector), threshold signatures can produce quorum signatures of size  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits (once a quorum has been generated, the individual identities of the signers are not needed for its verification).
Therefore, these implementations are of constant size and constant verification time, and thus succinct. 
 
 
 
 D.1.2  Verifying intermediary signatures without storing all individual public keys 

 
 As mentioned previously, only a single global public key is needed to verify a threshold signature, instead of all the signers’ public keys.
However, the coordinator must verify all intermediary signatures’ validity before generating the threshold signature.
To do that without having to store all the system’s public keys, it is possible to use a  vector commitment  ( VC )  [ 13 ] , a generalization of the classical commitment scheme presented in  Section   A.1 .
Like its name suggests, a VC scheme commits a vector of values, upon which it is then possible to have a “partial” opening of a single value of the vector, without having to open the vector in its entirety. One of the most widely known examples of vector commitment is Merkle trees  [ 35 ] . 
 
 
 In our context, this scheme would allow us to “compress” the entire vector of public keys of the system into a VC of size  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda) .
This VC would be created during the system setup.
In addition to the global VC, each correct process  p i p_{i}  also stores its individual public key, and the partial opening for the  i i th  value of the VC, corresponding to  p i p_{i} ’s public key.
This way,  p i p_{i}  can prove to another process  p j p_{j}  that it indeed belongs to the system, by sending to  p j p_{j}  its individual public key and partial opening.
With this data,  p j p_{j}  can then verify that the  i i th  value of the global VC indeed opens to  p i p_{i} ’s public key. 
 
 
 Since each correct process  p i p_{i}  stores the global VC ( O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits), its individual public key ( O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits), and the partial opening of the  i i th  value of the global VC ( O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits), this technique does not impact the overall storage cost of our AP algorithm ( Algorithm   4 ) of  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits stored per correct process. 
 
 
 More generally, this technique effectively allows us to have a public key infrastructure (PKI) under a fixed storage cost of  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits per correct process (provided that the set of public keys does not change over time).
For simplicity, all this machinery is hidden in the validation of intermediary signatures of  Algorithm   4 ,  algorithms   4  and  4 . 
 
 
 
 D.1.3  Efficiently aggregating intermediary signatures 

 
 For constructing a  k k -threshold signature, a coordinator process has to receive and aggregate  k k  intermediary signatures by  k k  distinct processes.
In particular, it has to make sure that it does not aggregate multiple intermediary signatures from the same process. 
 
 
 For the sake of simplicity, we address these issues by a naive approach in  Algorithm   4 : we require the coordinator  p i p_{i}  to save all the intermediary signatures it receives (and implicitly their signer’s identity) in the  𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑠 i \mathit{sigs}_{i}  set ( algorithm   4 ).
Once the threshold signature has been produced,  𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑠 i \mathit{sigs}_{i}  is emptied, and all the temporary data (intermediary signatures and sender identities) are deleted.
The  𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑠 i \mathit{sigs}_{i}  set contains at most  n n  intermediary signatures ( O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits) and signer identities ( O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n)  bits), which incurs a total of  O ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( λ + log ⁡ n ) ) O(n(\lambda+\log n))  bits.
However, as this data is stored only temporarily during the execution of the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( ) \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}()  operation, we consider that it pertains to the  space complexity  of  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( ) \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}() , and not the overall  storage cost  of our system. 
 
 
 Remark that this suboptimal space complexity can be easily reduced to  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits using the following method.
Instead of aggregating the intermediary signatures all at once at the end of the execution of  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( ) \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}()  ( algorithm   4 ), they can be gradually aggregated as they are received ( algorithm   4 ) on an aggregated signature of  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits, which becomes a threshold signature once it aggregates at least  k k  intermediary signatures.
This way, each intermediary signature can be forgotten right after it is received and aggregated.
Furthermore, the coordinator can use a bit vector of  n n  bits to efficiently keep track of the processes that already participated in the aggregated signature (the  i i th  bit indicates if the intermediary signature of  p i p_{i}  was already received or not). 
 
 
 
 
 D.2  Algorithm 

 
 In this section, we present our AP implementation, which eschews consensus by using threshold signatures for quorums.
It achieves lightness by using succinct cryptographic primitives and ensuring a storage cost of  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits per correct process, and a communication of  O ⁡ ( λ ​ n ) O(\lambda n)  bits sent overall. 16 16 
               16 
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            For these analyses, like in our asset transfer algorithm of  Algorithm   3 , we impose that sequence numbers are constant-size. 
 
 
 Algorithm   4  describes the code of this implementation for a correct process  p i p_{i}  and a AP predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}} .
It uses 3 local variables:  𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑠 i \mathit{sigs}_{i}  (a set of intermediary signatures used for generating threshold signatures),  v i v_{i}  (the value of the current  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  execution), and  𝑠𝑒𝑞 ​ _ ​ 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑠 i \mathit{seq\_nums}_{i}  (the array containing the sequence number (initialized with  0 0 ) of each process  p j p_{j} ,  j ∈ [ n ] j\in[n] ). 
 
 
 The  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 \mathsf{ap\_verify}  operation simply verifies that the provided AP  σ j \sigma_{j}  is a valid  ( ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 ) (\lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor{+}1) -threshold signature for the provided value  v v , sequence number  𝑠𝑛 j \mathit{sn}_{j}  and process  p j p_{j}  ( algorithm   4 ). 
 
 
 The  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  operation first computes its next sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  of  p i p_{i}  ( algorithm   4 ).
Then, it verifies that the provided  v v ,  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data} , and  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  satisfy the AP predicate ( algorithm   4 ).
If it does,  p i p_{i}  then saves the provided value  v v  in the local variable  v i v_{i}  ( algorithm   4 ).
Next,  p i p_{i}  generates the first signature of its payload, called the initialization signature ( algorithm   4 ), and broadcasts it in a  quorumInit  message which requests other processes of the system to sign its payload ( algorithm   4 ).
Process  p i p_{i}  then waits for a quorum of intermediary signatures from other processes ( algorithm   4 ), and when it is received,  p i p_{i}  aggregates all intermediary signatures into a  ( ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 ) (\lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor{+}1) -threshold signature ( algorithm   4 ), flushes the temporary data ( algorithm   4 ) and returns the AP and next sequence number ( algorithm   4 ). 
 
 
 When  p i p_{i}  receives a  quorumInit  message from a process  p j p_{j} , it first checks that the provided initialization signature is valid ( algorithm   4 ) and that the provided  v v ,  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data} , and  𝑠𝑛 j \mathit{sn}_{j}  satisfy the AP predicate ( algorithm   4 ).
Then,  p i p_{i}  waits for the provided  𝑠𝑛 j \mathit{sn}_{j}  to be the next one (in FIFO order) to be processed ( algorithm   4 ).
After that,  p i p_{i}  produces its intermediary signature ( algorithm   4 ) and sends it to  p j p_{j}  in a  quorumSig  message ( algorithm   4 ).
Finally,  p i p_{i}  updates the sequence number of  p j p_{j}  in  𝑠𝑒𝑞 ​ _ ​ 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑠 i \mathit{seq\_nums}_{i}  with its new  𝑠𝑛 j \mathit{sn}_{j}  ( algorithm   4 ). 
 
 
 When  p i p_{i}  receives a  quorumSig  message, it checks if the intermediary signature is valid ( algorithm   4 ), if it has not already received a previous intermediary signature from the same signer ( algorithm   4 ), and if it has not already produced an AP for the payload ( algorithm   4 ).
If these conditions pass,  p i p_{i}  saves the provided intermediary signature ( algorithm   4 ).

 D.3  Proof of Algorithm  4 

 
 In the following correctness proofs of the AP scheme, we consider an AP object  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A}  set up with an AP predicate  P A \mathit{P_{A}} . 
 
 
 Lemma D.1   (AP-Validity) . 
 
 If  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for a value  v v  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from a correct process  p i p_{i} , then  p i p_{i}  has executed  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ( v , ⋆ ) / σ i .\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\star)/\sigma_{i}  as its  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i} th  invocation of  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ( . . ) .\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(..) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let us assume that  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for value  v v  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from a correct process  p i p_{i} .
By definition of the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 \mathsf{ap\_verify}  operation at  algorithm   4 ,  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid  ( ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 ) (\lfloor\frac{n{+}t}{2}\rfloor+1) -threshold signature for  v v  at  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from  p i p_{i} .
This threshold signature must have been aggregated from at least  ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 \lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor+1  intermediary signatures.
Given the system assumption  n > 3 ​ t n>3t , we have  ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 > ⌊ 4 ​ t 2 ⌋ + 1 = 2 ​ t + 1 ≥ t + 1 \lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor+1>\lfloor\frac{4t}{2}\rfloor+1=2t+1\geq t+1 .
Therefore, at least one correct process must have produced an intermediary signature for  ⟨ v , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ⟩ \langle v,\mathit{sn}_{i},i\rangle  at  algorithm   4 .
However, to execute this line, this correct process must have verified the initialization signature of  p i p_{i}  at  algorithm   4 .
By the unforgeability of signatures, the only way to produce this initialization signature is for  p i p_{i}  to execute  algorithm   4 , during an  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ( v , ⋆ ) / σ i .\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\star)/\sigma_{i}  execution.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma D.2   (AP-Agreement) . 
 
 There are no two different valid APs  σ i \sigma_{i}  and  σ i ′ \sigma_{i}^{\prime}  for two different values  v v  and  v ′ v^{\prime}  at the same sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  and from the same prover  p i p_{i} .
More formally,  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ i , v , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ) = 𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 ​ ( σ i ′ , v ′ , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{i},v,\mathit{sn}_{i},i)=\mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_verify}$}(\sigma_{i}^{\prime},v^{\prime},\mathit{sn}_{i},i)=\mathtt{true}  implies  v = v ′ v=v^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let us assume, on the contrary, that there exists two different valid APs  σ i \sigma_{i}  and  σ i ′ \sigma_{i}^{\prime}  for two different values  v v  and  v ′ v^{\prime}  at the same sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  and from the same process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty).
By definition of the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 \mathsf{ap\_verify}  operation at  algorithm   4 ,  σ i \sigma_{i}  and  σ i ′ \sigma_{i}^{\prime}  are valid  ( ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 ) (\lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor{+}1) -threshold signatures for  v v  and  v ′ v^{\prime}  (resp.) at  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from  p i p_{i} .
These threshold signatures must have been aggregated from the intermediary signatures of two sets of processes,  A A  and  B B , which have respectively signed  ⟨ v , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ⟩ \langle v,\mathit{sn}_{i},i\rangle  and  ⟨ v ′ , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ⟩ \langle v^{\prime},\mathit{sn}_{i},i\rangle .
We have  | A | ≥ ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 ≤ | B | |A|\geq\lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor+1\leq|B| , or equivalently,  | A | > n + t 2 < | B | |A|>\frac{n+t}{2}<|B| . 17 17 
                   17 
                  
                  
                  
                  
                  
                  
                  
                Recall that  ∀ ⁡ i ∈ ℤ , r ∈ ℝ : ( i ≥ ⌊ r ⌋ + 1 ) ⇔ ( i > r ) \Forall i\in\mathbb{Z},r\in\mathbb{R}:(i\geq\lfloor r\rfloor+1)\iff(i>r) . 
We thus have  | A ∩ B | = | A | + | B | − | A ∪ B | > 2 ​ n + t 2 − | A ∪ B | ≥ 2 ​ n + t 2 − n = t |A\cap B|=|A|+|B|-|A\cup B|>2\frac{n+t}{2}-|A\cup B|\geq 2\frac{n+t}{2}-n=t .
Therefore, at least one correct process  p j p_{j}  must belong both to  A A  and  B B , and must have signed both  ⟨ v , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ⟩ \langle v,\mathit{sn}_{i},i\rangle  and  ⟨ v ′ , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ⟩ \langle v^{\prime},\mathit{sn}_{i},i\rangle  for  v ≠ v ′ v\neq v^{\prime} .
But by the fact that correct processes produce intermediary signatures for some  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  at  algorithm   4  and right after updating the  i i -th sequence number in their  𝑠𝑒𝑞 ​ _ ​ 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑠 \mathit{seq\_nums}  array at  algorithm   4 , it follows that correct processes produce at most one intermediary signature for a given  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  and sender identity  i i , which contradicts the fact that  p i p_{i}  must belong both to  A A  and  B B .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma D.3   (AP-Knowledge-Soundness) . 
 
 If  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for value  v v  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i > 0 \mathit{sn}_{i}>0  from a prover  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty), then  p i p_{i}  knows some  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  such that  P A ​ ( ⋆ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) / 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(\star,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})/\mathtt{true} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let us assume that  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for value  v v  at sequence number  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from a process  p i p_{i}  (correct or faulty).
By definition of the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗏𝖾𝗋𝗂𝖿𝗒 \mathsf{ap\_verify}  operation at  algorithm   4 ,  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid  ( ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 ) (\lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor{+}1) -threshold signature for  v v  at  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from  p i p_{i} .
This threshold signature must have been aggregated from at least  ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 \lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor+1  intermediary signatures.
Given the system assumption  n > 3 ​ t n>3t , we have  ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 > ⌊ 4 ​ t 2 ⌋ + 1 = 2 ​ t + 1 ≥ t + 1 \lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor+1>\lfloor\frac{4t}{2}\rfloor+1=2t+1\geq t+1 .
Therefore, at least one correct process must have produced an intermediary signature for  ⟨ v , 𝑠𝑛 i , i ⟩ \langle v,\mathit{sn}_{i},i\rangle  at  algorithm   4 .
However, to execute this line, this correct process must have verified the satisfaction of  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  by the received  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  at  algorithm   4 , which entails that the prover  p i p_{i}  must have known the  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  such that  P A ​ ( ⋆ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) / 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(\star,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})/\mathtt{true} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma D.4   (AP-Termination) . 
 
 Given a correct process  p i p_{i}  that executes  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\mathit{data})/r  with value  v v  as its  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i} th  invocation of  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ( . . ) .\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(..) , and a  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data} , if  P A ​ ( ⋆ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(\star,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})=\mathtt{true}  then  r = σ i r=\sigma_{i} , where  σ i \sigma_{i}  is a valid AP for  v v  at  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  from  p i p_{i} . If  P A ​ ( ⋆ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(\star,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})=\mathtt{false} , then  r = 𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 r=\mathtt{abort} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let us assume that a correct process  p i p_{i}  executes  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) / r \mathit{Obj}_{A}.\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\mathit{data})/r  with value  v v  and a  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  as its  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i} th  invocation of  𝑂𝑏𝑗 A \mathit{Obj}_{A} . 𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ( . . ) .\textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(..) .
If  P A ​ ( ⋆ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) = 𝚏𝚊𝚕𝚜𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(\star,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})=\mathtt{false} , then  p i p_{i}  passes the condition at  algorithm   4  and return  𝚊𝚋𝚘𝚛𝚝 \mathtt{abort} .
If  P A ​ ( ⋆ , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i ) = 𝚝𝚛𝚞𝚎 \mathit{P_{A}}(\star,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i})=\mathtt{true} ,  p i p_{i}  continues the execution, produces an initialization signature at  algorithm   4 , broadcasts a  quorumInit  message at  algorithm   4  and wait for a quorum of signatures from the system at  algorithm   4 . 
 
 
 Upon receiving this  quorumInit  message, each correct process  p j p_{j}  passes the conditions at  algorithm   4  (as  p i p_{i}  has correctly generated its initialization signature  𝑠𝑖𝑔 i \mathit{sig}_{i} ) and at  algorithm   4  (as  p i p_{i}  has verified the satisfaction of  P A \mathit{P_{A}}  by  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  at  algorithm   4 ), and then wait that the received  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  is the next in FIFO order to be processed at  algorithm   4 .
As  p i p_{i}  uses its sequence numbers in FIFO order, then  p i p_{i}  has necessarily sent a  quorumInit  message for each  𝑠𝑛 i ′ ∈ [ 1 . . 𝑠𝑛 i ] \mathit{sn}_{i}^{\prime}\in[1..\mathit{sn}_{i}] , and  p j p_{j}  will eventually receive all these  quorumInit  messages.
By induction,  p j p_{j}  will pass the  𝗐𝖺𝗂𝗍 \mathsf{wait}  statement at  algorithm   4  for each  𝑠𝑛 i ′ ∈ [ 1 . . 𝑠𝑛 i ] \mathit{sn}_{i}^{\prime}\in[1..\mathit{sn}_{i}] , because if  𝑠𝑛 i ′ = 1 \mathit{sn}_{i}^{\prime}=1 , the condition  algorithm   4  is satisfied as  𝑠𝑒𝑞 ​ _ ​ 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑠 j ​ [ i ] \mathit{seq\_nums}_{j}[i]  is initialized at  algorithm   4  to  0 0 , and if  𝑠𝑛 i ′ > 1 \mathit{sn}_{i}^{\prime}>1 , then  p j p_{j}  will eventually replace the  𝑠𝑛 i ′ − 2 \mathit{sn}_{i}^{\prime}-2  by  𝑠𝑛 i ′ − 1 \mathit{sn}_{i}^{\prime}-1  at  algorithm   4 .

Therefore, all correct processes, which are at least  n − t n-t , will eventually pass the  𝗐𝖺𝗂𝗍 \mathsf{wait}  statement at  algorithm   4 .
Given the system assumption  n > 3 ​ t n>3t , we have  n − t = 2 ​ n − 2 ​ t 2 > n + 3 ​ t − 2 ​ t 2 = n + t 2 n-t=\frac{2n-2t}{2}>\frac{n+3t-2t}{2}=\frac{n+t}{2} .
Therefore, strictly more than  n + t 2 \frac{n+t}{2}  (or, equivalently, at least  ⌊ n + t 2 ⌋ + 1 \lfloor\frac{n+t}{2}\rfloor+1 )
correct processes will produce an intermediary signature at  algorithm   4  and send it back to  p i p_{i}  at  algorithm   4 . 
 
 
 Finally,  p i p_{i}  will receive this quorum of intermediary signatures at  algorithm   4 , which will unlock the  𝗐𝖺𝗂𝗍 \mathsf{wait}  instruction at  algorithm   4 , and  p i p_{i}  will aggregate all intermediary signatures into a valid AP  σ i \sigma_{i} , and will return  ⟨ σ i , 𝑠𝑛 i ⟩ \langle\sigma_{i},\mathit{sn}_{i}\rangle  at  algorithm   4 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 D.4  Consensus-freedom of Algorithm  4 

 
 The consensus-freedom of  Algorithm   4  follows trivially from the fact that the only communication primitives that it uses are classic  𝗌𝖾𝗇𝖽 \mathsf{send} / 𝗋𝖾𝖼𝖾𝗂𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{receive}  operations and a best-effort  𝖻𝗋𝗈𝖺𝖽𝖼𝖺𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{broadcast}  operation, and because it always terminates in the presence of failures and asynchrony. 
 
 
 
 D.5  Succinctness of Algorithm  4 

 
 The succinctness of  Algorithm   4  comes from the succinctness of threshold signatures: as shown in  Section   D.1 , the best implementations of threshold signatures guarantee that the size and verification time of threshold signatures (and therefore also the agreement proofs  σ i \sigma_{i}  produced by  Algorithm   4 ) are equivalent to that of a single intermediary signature.
Therefore,  Algorithm   4  is succinct. 
 
 
 
 D.6  Storage cost of Algorithm  4 

 
 The storage of  Algorithm   4  is of  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits stored by each correct process.
It comes from its local variables: the  𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑠 i \mathit{sigs}_{i}  set and  v i v_{i}  values are emptied at the end of each  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove}  execution ( algorithm   4 ) 18 18 
               18 
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            See  Section   D.1.3  for a discussion on why we consider the storage of these temporary variables to be part of the space complexity of the  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( ) \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}()  operation (and not the system’s storage cost), and how this space complexity could be easily reduced to  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits.
 , and  𝑠𝑒𝑞 ​ _ ​ 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑠 i \mathit{seq\_nums}_{i}  is an array of size  n n  containing constant-size sequence numbers (by definition).
Lastly, the threshold signature scheme requires the storage of  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits (see  Section   D.1.2 ).
Therefore, each correct process only stores  O ⁡ ( λ + n ) O(\lambda+n)  bits in  Algorithm   4 . 
 
 
 
 D.7  Communication cost of Algorithm  4 

 
 The communication cost of  Algorithm   4  is  O ⁡ ( n ​ λ ) O(n\lambda)  bits sent by correct processes overall during an execution of  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 \mathsf{ap\_prove} .
It follows from the message exchanges entailed by an  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 ) \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(v,\mathit{data})  invocation by a correct prover  p i p_{i} .
Recall that, for simplicity, we assume that the size of the  v v  and  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data}  parameters of  𝖺𝗉 ​ _ ​ 𝗉𝗋𝗈𝗏𝖾 ( . . ) \textup{$\mathsf{ap\_prove}$}(..)  are of constant size. 
 
 
 In a first step,  p i p_{i}  broadcasts a  quorumInit ​ ( v , 𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 , 𝑠𝑛 i , i , 𝑠𝑖𝑔 i ) \textsc{quorumInit}(v,\mathit{data},\mathit{sn}_{i},i,\mathit{sig}_{i})  message at  algorithm   4 , where  v v ,  𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎 \mathit{data} , and  𝑠𝑛 i \mathit{sn}_{i}  are constant-size,  i i  has  O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n)  bits, and  𝑠𝑖𝑔 i \mathit{sig}_{i}  has  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits.
As this message is broadcast to all  n n  processes, the overall communication cost of this first step is  O ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( λ + log ⁡ n ) ) O(n(\lambda+\log n))  bits. 
 
 
 In a second step, every correct process of the system  p j p_{j}  receives the  quorumInit  message, passes the conditions at  algorithms   4  and  4 , and sends a  quorumSig ​ ( j , 𝑠𝑖𝑔 j ) \textsc{quorumSig}(j,\mathit{sig}_{j})  message to  p i p_{i}  at  algorithm   4 , where  j j  has  O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n)  bits, and  𝑠𝑖𝑔 j \mathit{sig}_{j}  has  O ⁡ ( λ ) O(\lambda)  bits.
This amounts to  O ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( λ + log ⁡ n ) ) O(n(\lambda+\log n))  bits sent overall by correct processes during this second step. 
 
 
 Therefore, the total number of bits sent by correct processes during the execution of  Algorithm   4  is  O ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( λ + log ⁡ n ) ) O(n(\lambda+\log n)) .
However, as we assume that  λ = Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) \lambda=\Omega(\log n)  (see  Section   3 , ¶  Security parameter  λ \lambda ), the previous asymptote simplifies to  O ⁡ ( n ​ λ ) O(n\lambda)  bits sent overall. 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix E  Trustless system setup 

 
 Like most distributed or cryptographic systems, our system requires a setup phase to compute the initial public and private parameters enabling processes to participate in the system.
We assume the initial knowledge of a genesis data structure to specify the process identifiers and the initial amounts of their respective accounts.
Our system is set up in a trustless manner,  i.e. , no trusted party is involved in the distributed computation of the system parameters nor holds a trapdoor or secret that could otherwise be used to compromise the system’s safety.
We refer to the distributed setup operation of the system as  𝗌𝗒𝗌𝗍𝖾𝗆 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗍𝗎𝗉 ​ ( ) \mathsf{system\_setup}() .
Note that the generation of the genesis data is not part of the  𝗌𝗒𝗌𝗍𝖾𝗆 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗍𝗎𝗉 ​ ( ) \mathsf{system\_setup}() .
This trustless setup is possible because the cryptographic schemes implementing our schemes also provide a trustless setup (see  Appendix   C ). 
 
 
 We provide the following implementation draft to demonstrate the feasibility of  𝗌𝗒𝗌𝗍𝖾𝗆 ​ _ ​ 𝗌𝖾𝗍𝗎𝗉 ​ ( ) \mathsf{system\_setup}() . 
 
 1.  
 
 Assume public knowledge of  𝗀𝖾𝗇𝖾𝗌𝗂𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝖽𝖺𝗍𝖺 ← ⋃ i = 1 n ⟨ p ​ k i , A i , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ⟩ \mathsf{genesis\_data}\leftarrow\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\langle pk_{i},A_{i},\mathit{bal\_c}_{i}\rangle , where  A i A_{i}  is the empty accumulator of process  p i p_{i}  and  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i \mathit{bal\_c}_{i}  is a commitment to the initial balance of  p i p_{i}  ( 𝚒𝚗𝚒𝚝 i \mathtt{init}_{i} ). 
 
 2.  
 
 Assume private knowledge of the opening of  𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i \mathit{bal\_c}_{i} , secret parameters of  A i A_{i}  and secret key of signature key pair  ⟨ s ​ k i , p ​ k i ⟩ \langle sk_{i},pk_{i}\rangle  for each process  p i p_{i} . 
 
 3.  
 
 Execute the setup operation of each cryptographic scheme. 
 
 4.  
 
 Generate a threshold signature  σ i \sigma_{i}  (compatible with  Algorithm   4 ) that will serve as the initial agreement proof of  p i p_{i}  for each  v i = ⟨ A i , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i ⟩ v_{i}=\langle A_{i},\mathit{bal\_c}_{i}\rangle . 
 
 5.  
 
 Each process  p i p_{i}  can now safely delete  𝗀𝖾𝗇𝖾𝗌𝗂𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝖽𝖺𝗍𝖺 \mathsf{genesis\_data}  and output its initial parameters  ⟨ A i , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ c i , σ i , 𝑏𝑎𝑙 ​ _ ​ o i ⟩ \langle A_{i},\mathit{bal\_c}_{i},\sigma_{i},\mathit{bal\_o}_{i}\rangle . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix F  Transfer batching 

 
 In this section, we briefly propose a method to commit to batches of transfers (instead of single transfers) and to verify those batches succinctly,  i.e. , faster than if the batched transfers were verified individually.
Such a method is advantageous both in terms of anonymity and efficiency, which is explained in more detail in  Section   6.2.5 . 
 
 
 Incrementally Verifiable Computation (IVC).   
Informally, an IVC can be represented as a function  F F  that takes as input a previous execution of  F F  and some additional input.
For example,  F F  could be the function implementing the operation  𝗉𝗋𝗈𝖼𝖾𝗌𝗌 ​ _ ​ 𝗍𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗌𝖿𝖾𝗋 ​ ( ) \textup{$\mathsf{process\_transfer}$}()  of  Algorithm   3 .
Then each execution of  F F  would take as input an accumulator, an agreement proof and a balance commitment of a process  p i p_{i}  and update the accumulator and balance commitment of  p i p_{i}  accordingly.
Note that by “chaining” several iterations of  F F , we obtain the processing of a batch of transfers. 
 
 
 Folding scheme for IVC proofs.   
Folding schemes such as Nova  [ 32 ]  or Sangria  [ 23 ]  allow the creation of efficient SNARKS that a given number of iterations of an IVC were correctly executed by generating a proof for each step, then combining them successively on-the-fly in constant time (factor of  2 2  for Nova).
Once the prover wishes to demonstrate the correct processing of its IVC iterations, the IVC proof is compressed into a single, small and succinct SNARK proof.

     Instructions for reporting errors 
     We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below: 
     
       Click the "Report Issue"  ( 
             
           )  button, located in the page header. 
     
     Tip:  You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. 
     Our team has already identified  the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all. 
     Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a  list of packages that need conversion , and welcome  developer contributions .
